A prerequisite for the direct mapping of users is that the corresponding user accounts must already be registered in the SAP ID service. If this requirement is not fulfilled, SAP Support Portal enables you to create S-users for your company.

To create S-users for global account users, you must log on to the https://support.sap.com/ page with an S-user for your company. This user must have the appropriate user administration permissions. Then, proceed with the following steps:

  1. In the SAP Support Portal menu, select the Manage Users entry in the menu to jump to user maintenance, as shown in Figure 3.3.

    Figure 3.3     SAP Support Portal Menu

  2. You’re now in the user overview, as shown in Figure 3.4.

    Figure 3.4     Creating a New S-User

    Click on the Request User button in the bottom toolbar to create a new user.

  3. In the creation dialog box, enter the details of the user, as shown in Figure 3.5.

    Figure 3.5     Maintaining User Details

  4. Finish the creation process by clicking Submit.

The S-user is not created immediately since SAP must first confirm its creation, which can take up to 24 hours. SAP also assigns the S-user ID during this process. Once approved, the user will receive an invitation email with instructions on activating the user account.

Prerequisites for managing users at the subaccount level include, first, logging on to the subaccount and, second, having sufficient permissions for user management. You can create a subaccount starting from your global account as described in Chapter 1, Section 1.3.2. The user who created the subaccount is automatically assigned the administrator and developer roles for the subaccount. Initially, only this user has access to the subaccount.

To create additional users on the subaccount level, (which are called members), switch to the user administration screen. For this task, select the Members menu item in the SAP BTP cockpit, as shown in Figure 3.6.

Figure 3.6     User Management Menu

On the user overview screen, you’ll see all users currently assigned to the subaccount, as shown in Figure 3.7. These users can be edited and deleted at this point. Only your own user cannot be deleted.

Figure 3.7     User Overview on the Subaccount Level

To add a new user to the subaccount, click on the Add Members button.

You can create up to 100 users at a time. In the User IDs field, specify the S-user ID(s) generated by the SAP ID service, as shown in Figure 3.8. The system does not check whether these IDs actually exist, nor whether the corresponding users are assigned to your company’s user master data in SAP Support Portal. Therefore, check the IDs carefully; otherwise, people from outside your company might be granted access.

Figure 3.8     Adding Users

In the same dialog box, you can assign roles to users. You can either use the standard roles delivered by SAP or use self-defined roles. We’ll discuss roles in more detail in Section 3.4.1.

After users have been successfully assigned, they will appear in the user overview with the note -subaccount not visited yet- in the Name column, as shown in Figure 3.9. Once a user has logged on to the subaccount for the first time, the name of the user maintained in the SAP ID service is used instead of this note.

Figure 3.9     New User in the User Overview

[»]  Disadvantages of the SAP ID Service for User Authentication

Using the SAP ID service to authenticate the users of a subaccount has some disadvantages. For example, you cannot store a validity period for a user in the subaccount. You also have no way to lock or unlock a user directly in the subaccount. In Section 3.3.2, we’ll show you how you can use the Identity Authentication service as an alternative way to authenticate users to remove these limitations.

On the user overview screen, you also have the option of viewing all changes made to users by clicking the History button. The change history for the subaccount used in this book is shown in Figure 3.10.

Figure 3.10     Change History for the Users of a Subaccount

How a user logs on to the subaccount depends on whether the SAP ID service is used for authentication or whether the Identity Authentication service takes on the role of platform identity provider for the subaccount. If the SAP ID service is used, users can log on with their user data via the URL https://account.hana.ondemand.com/. After successfully logging on, as a user, you’ll see an overview of all the global accounts to which you’re either directly assigned or in which you’re assigned at least via a subaccount, as shown in Figure 3.11.

Figure 3.11     Overview of Global Accounts

If you navigate to the SAP BTP cockpit of the global account, navigate to Subaccounts • System Landscape to call up the overview of all subaccounts to which you’re assigned, as shown in Figure 3.12.

Figure 3.12     Overview of Subaccounts

When a user has logged on to the subaccount for the first time, the user name is read and stored in the user overview of the subaccount, as shown in Figure 3.13.

Figure 3.13     Subaccount User after First Logon

You can delete a user by clicking on the delete icon in the Actions column for a user’s entry, as shown in Figure 3.14. This action is logged and can be viewed via the change history.

SAP BTP also offers the option of deleting more than one user at a time. For this task, select the desired users and then click the Delete button, as shown in Figure 3.15. This action is logged just like the deletion of a single user and can be viewed via the change history.

Figure 3.14     Deleting an Individual User

Figure 3.15     Deleting Multiple Users

3.3    Trust Configuration

In Security Assertion Markup Language (SAML) 2.0-based communication, a subaccount of SAP BTP always acts in the role of the service provider (see Chapter 2, Section 2.2.1). Each subaccount represents a separate service provider. If you have a requirement to represent each of your applications by a separate service provider, you must therefore create and configure a separate subaccount for each application.

Using SAML 2.0 requires that a trust relationship (sometimes shortened to “trust”) be established between the service provider and the identity provider. The service provider is configured in the subaccount. For this task, navigate to Security • Trust in the SAP BTP cockpit. We’ll show you how to set up a local service provider in the next section.

You must also configure the identity provider. In addition to using the SAP ID service as an identity provider, which is activated in the standard system, the subaccount allows you to configure an application identity provider or a platform identity provider (see also Chapter 2, Section 2.2.1). You’ll learn how to set up platform identity providers and application identity providers in Section 3.3.2 and Section 3.3.3, respectively.

3.3.1    Setting Up the Local Service Provider

The trust settings of the local service provider inherit the default configuration from SAP BTP. Among other things, in the trust settings, you’ll define that the SAP ID service is to be used as the identity provider. In the documentation for SAP BTP, Neo environment, you’ll find a note that this default setting should only be used for testing and for exploring the available scenarios. Therefore, for productive scenarios, you should configure a local service provider according to your requirements.

The Local Service Provider tab in the Trust configuration allows you to make the following settings, as shown in Figure 3.16:

  • Configuration Type

  • Principal Propagation

  • Force Authentication

Figure 3.16     Local Service Provider Configuration

The Configuration Type field allows the following configuration types for the local service provider:

  • Default
    With this setting, the trust settings of the local service provider inherit the default configuration from SAP BTP.

  • None
    With this type, no trust settings are defined for the local service provider, and thus, the service provider does not participate in any identity federation scenario.

  • Custom
    With this type, you can define a specific configuration for the local service provider that differs from the default configuration.

The Principal Propagation field allows you to control whether user identity information is shared between applications. You can select either Enabled or Disabled. Set the value to Enabled if you want to enable cross-application single sign-on (SSO). If you want to use SAP Web IDE for your developments, the Principal Propagation field must be set to Enabled, which is the SAP default.

With the Force Authentication field, you can force users to reauthenticate themselves when starting SAP BTP. If you’ve configured an SSO, this property does not apply at runtime. You can select either Enabled or Disabled for this field. By default, the value Default is set, which is equivalent to Disabled.

3.3.2    Setting Up the Platform Identity Provider

The only alternative platform identity provider that can be used is the Identity Authentication service that we introduced in Chapter 2, Section 2.4.1. This service allows you to authenticate the users of your subaccount. By default, the SAP ID service is set as the platform identity provider. To use your own instance of the Identity Authentication service, click the Use Identity Authentication Tenant link under the Platform Identity Provider tab on the Trust Management screen, as shown in Figure 3.17.

Figure 3.17     Activating the Platform Identity Provider

Next, select an Identity Authentication tenant associated with your global account, as shown in Figure 3.18, and click Save to save your selection.

Figure 3.18     Selecting an Identity Authentication Tenant

A few seconds will be required to assign the tenant. This step establishes a trust relationship with the Identity Authentication service, and the subaccount is registered as an application in this service, where it can be further configured.

After the mapping has been successfully completed, the Identity Authentication service is visible on the Trust Management screen as a platform identity provider. From this screen, you can jump to the SAP BTP cockpit by clicking the Cockpit button, as shown in Figure 3.19.

Figure 3.19     Identity Authentication Service Displayed as a Platform Identity Provider

For users to be authenticated using the Identity Authentication service, they must be created in the Identity Authentication service, as described in Chapter 2, Section 2.4.1. This step allows authentication to be successfully performed, but access to the subaccount by these users is still not yet possible. Users must also be added as members to the list of subaccount users, as described in Section 3.2.

Now, users can authenticate and access the subaccount. The subaccount is accessed in SAP BTP, Neo environment using the following URL:

https://account-<SubaccountName>.<Datacenter>.hana.ondemand.com/cockpit

After successful logon, you’ll see the list of global accounts, as shown in Figure 3.20. From this list, you can jump to the desired subaccount, as described in Section 3.2.

Figure 3.20     Identity Authentication Service: Overview of Global Accounts after Successful Logon

On the user overview screen in the subaccount, notice that the properties of your user have been filled with the data maintained in the Identity Authentication service, as shown in Figure 3.21.

Figure 3.21     Identity Authentication Service: User Overview with User Data

No further settings are required in the Identity Authentication tenant. However, you have the option of performing a more advanced configuration. For example, you might set up risk-based authentication, customize email templates to meet your needs, configure your own email server to send emails, or create your own theme for the Identity Authentication service logon screen.

Log on to the administration console for the Identity Authentication tenant and navigate to Applications & Resources • Applications.

Look for the technical name of the subaccount. You’ll find one or two entries. If you find two entries, the Identity Authentication tenant has been configured as both a platform identity provider and an application identity provider. When the tenant is enabled as a platform identity provider, the subaccount is created as a customer-owned application (custom application) named SAP Cloud Platform <technical name of subaccount> in the Identity Authentication tenant, as shown in Figure 3.22.

Figure 3.22     Registered Subaccount in the Platform Identity Provider

The subaccount assumes the role of the service provider in the SAML context. In the Identity Authentication service, all service providers are managed as applications, so you’ll also find the subaccount on this screen, which allows you to configure each service provider that uses the Identity Authentication service differently.

Some advanced configuration options for the service provider are shown in Figure 3.23. You can also maintain your own terms of use and privacy policies in different languages at this point, in addition to the advanced configurations mentioned earlier.

Figure 3.23     Service Provider: Advanced Configuration Options

3.3.3    Setting Up the Application Identity Provider

The application identity provider provides the user base for applications in your SAP BTP subaccount. For example, you can use your existing corporate identity provider to authenticate users for your applications and services. This scenario is called identity federation. SAP BTP uses SAML 2.0 for this purpose.

You don’t have to limit yourself to a single application identity provider; you can use multiple identity providers. In such a case, make sure that all user identities are unique across all identity providers and that no user is available in more than one identity provider. Otherwise, security roles could be incorrectly assigned in the SAP BTP subaccount.

SAP BTP also offers the option of using the Identity Authentication tenant as an application identity provider. Its configuration requires a little effort:

  1. In the SAP BTP cockpit, navigate to the Trust Management screen and open the Application Identity Provider tab.

  2. Click on the Add Identity Authentication Tenant link, as shown in Figure 3.24.

    Figure 3.24     Application Identity Provider Configuration

  3. Select the desired Identity Authentication tenant, as shown in Figure 3.25.

    Figure 3.25     Selecting an Identity Authentication Tenant

  4. The configuration process takes a few seconds. Now, the Identity Authentication tenant is configured as the application identity provider for the subaccount, as shown in Figure 3.26.

Figure 3.26     Identity Authentication Tenant as Application Identity Provider

In the Identity Authentication tenant, the subaccount is created as a customer-owned application. This application has the name of the Identity Authentication tenant, as shown in Figure 3.27. You can only tell that this application is the subaccount by the technical name embedded in the URL.

Figure 3.27     Subaccount in the Identity Authentication Tenant

As an alternative to using the Identity Authentication tenant, you can use any SAML 2.0-based identity provider as the subaccount’s application identity provider. To establish a trust relationship between the subaccount in the SAML service provider role and the identity provider, it is necessary to exchange metadata between the two actors. First, export the metadata of the service provider. In SAP BTP, Neo environment, you need to perform these steps in Trust Management:

  1. Navigate to the Trust Management area in the SAP BTP cockpit.

  2. Open the Local Service Provider tab.

  3. Set the Configuration Type field to Custom, which is a prerequisite for downloading the metadata.

  4. Save the configuration.

  5. The Get Metadata link will appear, as shown in Figure 3.28. Click on this link to download the metadata in the form of an XML file.

    Figure 3.28     Downloading Metadata for Establishing the Trust Relationship

  6. The next step is to import the subaccount metadata into the identity provider. How this import is performed depends on the third-party identity provider you choose, so we won’t go into specifics in this book. We show you how to perform this step in Section 3.6.2 using Microsoft Azure AD as an example.

  7. After the metadata has been imported, the specific configuration of the identity provider must be performed, which depends on the provider you choose. After that step, you can download the metadata from the identity provider.

  8. Importing the identity provider metadata into the subaccount is performed in Trust Management under the Application Identity Provider tab. Click the Add Trusted Identity Provider link, as shown in Figure 3.29.

    Figure 3.29     Adding a Trusted Identity Provider

  9. In the Trusted Identity Provider dialog box, click the Browse button, as shown in Figure 3.30, and select the metadata file of the identity provider on your local computer.

    Figure 3.30     Importing Identity Provider Metadata

  10. Optionally, you can still configure the desired default values and mappings for attributes and groups of SAP BTP and identity provider users under the Attributes and Groups tabs.

  11. After you’ve saved your configuration, the selected identity provider can be used as the application identity provider.

[»]  Cloud Security Concept When Connecting the Identity Authentication Service

For cost reasons, maintaining users in a central directory and connecting them via the Identity Authentication service is the first choice.

However, considerations with regard to security must be addressed. For example, you must clarify how many incorrect logons in the cloud will result in a user being blocked from the company network. You should also consider not making administrative and normal accounts directly identifiable by their names. This discretion will make it much harder for hackers to target administrative accounts. Many organizations tie in their directory services via the Identity Authentication service. However, this setup requires a sound cloud security concept. Among other things, we strongly recommend two-factor authentication.

3.4    Authorization Management

In Chapter 2, we introduced you to authentication and authorization. Identity providers can be used to authenticate users. Thus, a subaccount of SAP BTP does not perform authentication itself but instead delegates this task to an identity provider. As described earlier, this identity provider can be the SAP ID service managed by SAP, your Identity Authentication tenant, or the identity provider of a third-party provider.

Authorization, on the other hand, takes place directly in the subaccount. This process checks whether users have the necessary authorizations to perform a particular action.

3.4.1    Creating and Managing Roles

In SAP BTP, a distinction is made between application roles and platform roles regarding authorizations. Application roles are for activities in applications and subscribed services, while platform roles are for activities related to the administration of the subaccount.

Platform Roles

SAP delivers several predefined platform roles to authorize typical interactions with the platform. In addition, as a subaccount admin, you can combine different scopes to create a custom platform role that meets your individual requirements. Scopes are sets of permissions that define what activities users can perform in the subaccount and what platform resources they can access. A platform role is a collection of permissions, or scopes, that are managed by the platform at the subaccount level.

SAP includes the following predefined platform roles:

  • Administrator

  • Cloud connector admin

  • Developer

  • Support user

  • Application user admin

Predefined roles cannot be deleted or modified. If a role does not meet your requirements, you can copy it and make the desired adjustments. Alternatively, you can create a new role and add the desired permissions.

The administrator platform role authorizes you to perform the following tasks:

  • Manage subaccount users

  • Manage subscriptions, trust relationships, permissions, and OAuth settings

  • View and download heap dumps (memory images of the runtime environment)

  • Start and stop the SAP HANA service

  • View the Connectivity tab in the SAP BTP cockpit

  • Manage all authorizations of the developer role except for the debugging authorization

The cloud connector admin platform role authorizes you to perform the following tasks:

  • Open a secure tunnel in the cloud connector to connect your on-premise network and underlying systems to a subaccount of SAP BTP

  • Display the Connectivity tab in the SAP BTP cockpit

The developer platform role is assigned to newly created platform users by default and entitles you to perform the following tasks:

  • Deploy applications

  • Start and stop applications

  • Debug applications

  • Change log levels (i.e., the scope of logging for debugging applications)

  • Monitoring applications and their resource consumption

The support user platform role is designed for technical support and authorizes you to read subaccount-related data, such as metadata, configuration, and logs.

The application user admin platform role authorizes you to manage permissions for accessing HTML5 and Java applications at the application level and to manage permissions at the subscription level. This role does not authorize you to manage roles at the subaccount level nor to start, stop, or delete applications.

The administration of the platform roles is performed in the SAP BTP cockpit. In the cockpit, click on the Platform Roles entry in the menu to call up the administration environment for platform roles, as shown in Figure 3.31.

Figure 3.31     Calling Up the Administration Environment for Platform Roles

Click on the New Platform Role button to create a new platform role, as shown in Figure 3.32. Note that platform roles created in a subaccount of SAP BTP, Neo environment are available in all subaccounts created in the same global account and region. In other words, you cannot create a platform role that is visible only in one subaccount. A platform role can also be modified and deleted from other subaccounts.

Figure 3.32     Creating a Platform Role

In the dialog box for creating a new platform role, you must assign an ID. This ID is subject to certain restrictions and may not contain spaces, for example. The entry in the Display Name field is optional, but we recommend maintaining a meaningful display name in addition to the role ID, as shown in Figure 3.33.

Figure 3.33     Creating a Platform Role: Additional Options

Finally, you must assign the desired scopes to the role. An overview of the available scopes can be found in the documentation for SAP BTP at http://s-prs.de/v809805. SAP provides scopes in the following categories:

  • Account management

  • Agent activation for Dynatrace service

  • Authorization management

  • Connectivity service

  • Document service

  • Enterprise messaging

  • Extension integration management

  • Git service

  • HTML5 application management

  • Java application lifecycle management

  • Keystore service

  • Logging service

  • Member management

  • Metering service

  • Monitoring service

  • Multitarget application (MTA) management

  • OAuth client management

  • Password storage

  • SAP HANA/SAP ASE service

  • Service management

  • Subscription management

  • Trust management

  • Virtual machines

After selecting the desired scopes, save your platform role settings by clicking the Save button.

[+]  Developing an Authorization Concept

We recommend that you define the roles you require as part of developing an authorization concept. In this process, you should determine whether the standard roles can fulfill your requirements. Note at this point that the standard administrator and developer roles have quite extensive authorizations. You cannot combine platform roles and application roles.

After creation, the custom platform role is displayed on the platform roles overview screen, as shown in Figure 3.34. Unlike the predefined roles, no information icon is displayed for a custom platform role. You would click this icon to jump to the documentation for predefined roles. While predefined roles cannot be edited or deleted, this restriction does not exist for custom roles, which can be edited, displayed, copied, or deleted using the icons in the Actions column.

Figure 3.34     Overview of Platform Roles

The assignment of roles to users is performed via the user overview screen. For this task, navigate to the user overview screen via the Members menu item and click on the Edit icon , as shown in Figure 3.35, in the row of the user to whom you want to assign the role.

Figure 3.35     Editing a User

In the edit view for the user, all the available platform roles are listed. Select the roles you want to assign to the user (e.g., the custom role SAP Security Role we just created), as shown in Figure 3.36.

Figure 3.36     Performing a Role Assignment

No option for bulk editing exists, and thus, you must assign roles to each user individually. Note that determining all the users assigned to a role is not possible.

[»]  Assignment of Platform Roles

Which users should be assigned which platform roles depends on the use case. Developers and administrators are relatively easy to classify in this context. On a production subaccount, the cloud connector admin role is more likely to be assigned to a member of the Basis team; on a development subaccount, this role can also be assigned to developers.

Application Roles

Application roles are provided by various SAP BTP services, which are used to assign authorizations at the application level and have no effect on administrative activities in the SAP BTP subaccount.

[»]  Services, Subscriptions, and Applications

In the context of SAP BTP, we often talk about applications and subscriptions. The difference is easy to explain: A subscription runs in a different subaccount, called the provider account, and is designed to be used by the different subaccounts of various users, called consumer accounts. A subscription is a multiclient application that ensures that the data of individual consumers is strictly separated and isolated from each other. A subscription can be based on a Java application or on an HTML5 application.

Unlike a subscription, a service (in the form of an application) is provided directly in the customer’s subaccount. In simple terms, imagine that, when you activate a service, SAP installs the underlying application in your subaccount.

You can jump to the subscription management screen by navigating to Applications • Subscriptions, as shown in Figure 3.37.

Figure 3.37     Subscriptions Menu

On the subscriptions overview screen, Java applications and HTML applications are displayed separately. A technical name is displayed for each application, as shown in Figure 3.38. Click the name of an application in the Application column to jump to its details.

Figure 3.38     Overview of Subscriptions

In the details of an application, you’ll find an overview of the application metadata. In addition, you can maintain destinations and roles at this point.

Select the Roles menu item to jump to the application role administration screen, as shown in Figure 3.39.

Figure 3.39     Menu in the Detailed View of an Application

As an example, let’s manage some roles for the SAP Cloud Portal service (the Java application nwc). When activating this application, whether it is a service or a subscription is not obvious. You can find this information in its documentation. The SAP Cloud Portal service is delivered in the form of a subscription and provides four predefined roles.

[»]  Customer-Specific Application Roles

Theoretically, you could also create customer-specific roles at this level, which may not make sense at first glance because permissions must be checked in the application and this is only done for predefined roles. However, if you take a closer look at the SAP Cloud Portal service, you’ll see that custom roles can also be quite useful for subscriptions.

You can assign either individual users or user groups to a role. Click the Assign button above the Individual Users table on the roles overview screen to start assigning an individual user, as shown in Figure 3.40.

In the popup window that opens, you must manually maintain the User ID, as shown in Figure 3.41. No value or search help is offered, and no check is made to ensure if a user with this ID actually exists. Since these are application roles, you also have the option of assigning users that are not listed in the user overview list for the subaccount. After you’ve entered a user ID, confirm the assignment by clicking Assign.

Figure 3.40     Overview of Roles on the Application Level

Figure 3.41     Assigning a User to an Application Role

[+]  Using User Groups

We generally advise against assigning users directly to application roles in this way. If you have a large number of users, a lot of work may be required both for the initial setup of the application and during operations. Instead, we recommend using user groups, which we’ll discuss in more detail in Section 3.4.2.

For consumers of a service, no major differences exist compared to role management at the subscription level. However, for SAP, as the operator of SAP BTP, a significant difference is in play: Subscriptions only need to be updated in the provider account (i.e., updates are applied at a central location). In the case of a service, on the other hand, updates must be applied to all customer subaccounts.

To jump to the role maintenance of a service, select the Services entry in the Applications menu area, as shown in Figure 3.42. We’ll show you the role administration at this level in the following using the SAP Workflow Management service as an example. We have already activated this service. Click on the Workflow tile to jump to the service overview. If this tile is not visible in your subaccount, you should check whether you’ve licensed the service and assigned it to your subaccount at the global account level.

Figure 3.42     Service Overview on the Subaccount Level

On the workflow service overview page, you’ll see the Take Action section, as shown in Figure 3.43. Click the Configure Service link to jump to the configuration. Basically, this area is similar for all services, and you’ll always find a link to jump to the service configuration.

On the service configuration screen, select the Roles menu item to navigate to the role maintenance of the service.

Figure 3.43     Actions for a Service: Jumping to Configuration

As with subscriptions, you can assign either individual users or groups to a role. For this task, first select the desired role and then click the Assign button in the Individual Users area, as shown in Figure 3.44. Then, you must enter the user ID of the user. No option exists for mass assignment; you must assign each user individually. Therefore, we recommend creating user groups for this purpose as well. On this screen, too, you have the option of creating customer-specific roles.

Figure 3.44     Application Role User Assignment

For application roles, you can assign a role to a user via authorization management at the subaccount level. This option does not exist for platform roles. Up to now, we’ve only covered the reverse way, in which a user is assigned to a role. Access the Authorization Management via the menu path Security • Authorizations, as shown in Figure 3.45.

Figure 3.45     Jumping to Authorization Management in the Menu

Now, you can search for users. For this task, enter a user ID in the User input field and then click on the Show Assignments button, as shown in Figure 3.46. You’ll now see a list of all roles assigned to this user. Click the Assign button to assign a new role to the user.

Figure 3.46     Assigned Roles in Authorization Management

Role assignment occurs on three levels. First, you must select the subaccount. Then, you must specify the application on the second level, followed by the role on the third level. Save the assignment by clicking the Save button. As shown in Figure 3.47, we’ve assigned the WorkflowDeveloper role for the bpmworkflowruntime application to the user in the bpm subaccount.

Figure 3.47     Role Assignment in Authorization Management

At this point, note that the workflow service is also provided as a subscription, even though it is not visible in either the Java application overview or among the HTML5 applications. Services basically run in the same subaccount. Since the workflow service is provided in the bpm subaccount, this service must be a subscription.

After you’ve completed the role assignment settings, the new role is visible in the user’s role overview list, as shown in Figure 3.48. If you want to unassign the role, click the Unassign button next to the desired role.

Figure 3.48     Deleting a Role Assignment

The deletion of the role assignment must be confirmed in a dialog box.

[»]  Authorization Concept for Cloud and On-Premise Systems

Make sure that the permissions assigned for the cloud and for your local authorization concept match. For example, someone who is allowed to maintain users in the cloud should also be allowed in on-premise systems, and vice versa.

3.4.2    Creating and Managing User Groups

You can use user groups to simplify role assignments. You do not assign roles to individual users, but to a group. You can then assign individual users to the group, which thus allows you to make assignments and changes to authorizations from a central point.

Groups also offer an advantage in combination with application-specific identity providers: You have the option of using groups in mapping rules and can thus assign groups dynamically at runtime based on attributes supplied in the SAML assertion. We’ll show you this procedure using an example in Section 3.6.2.

To create a new user group, follow these steps:

  1. Navigate to Authorization Management via the menu path Security • Authorizations.

  2. Open the Groups tab and click the New Group button, as shown in Figure 3.49.

    Figure 3.49     Creating a New Group

  3. Assign a name for the group, as shown in Figure 3.50. You should use a descriptive name and make sure that the name is unique. Groups are created exclusively in the respective subaccount.

    Figure 3.50     Maintaining Group Names

  4. Save the group by clicking the Save button.

After the group has been created, you’ll see it on the group overview screen. On this screen, you can also assign roles or individual users to the group. Simply click on the Assign button above the Roles or Individual Users tables, as shown in Figure 3.51.

Figure 3.51     Assigning Roles and Users

The assignment of roles is shown in Figure 3.52. We discussed this dialog box earlier in Section 3.4.1. In this case, too, you’ll select a subaccount, an application, and a role to assign to the group and then save your selections by clicking the Save button.

Figure 3.52     Assigning a Role to a Group

Assigning a user to a group is shown in Figure 3.53. The dialog box is identical to the dialog box for assigning a user to an application role. Again, enter the user ID and click Save.

Figure 3.53     Assigning a User to a Group

After you’ve assigned the desired users and roles to a group, this information is visible on the group overview screen in the Roles and Individual Users tables, as shown in Figure 3.54. For this task, select the group in the upper area of the group overview. If necessary, you can delete the assignment of a role or a user by clicking the Unassign button in the corresponding row.

Figure 3.54     Result of Assigning Role and User

The group assignment is also visible at the service level. After you’ve assigned the WorkflowDeveloper role to the SAP_Security_Book group for the workflow service, you’ll see the role on the role overview screen of the workflow service, in the Groups table, as shown in Figure 3.55. The user assignment to the group is not visible at this point.

Figure 3.55     Checking Group Assignments in the Service

3.5    Checklist for Security and Permissions in SAP BTP, Neo Environment

Table 3.2 provides a checklist for permissions and entitlement management in SAP BTP, Neo environment.

Completed

Task

You have an overview of the different data centers for SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment and SAP BTP, Neo environment, and have noted the servers used in a central location.

You’ve determined which authentication method to use. Specific authentication processes can be set up for applications running in subaccounts in SAP BTP, Neo environment. This option is particularly useful if these applications are shared with a separate group of people, including externally managed identities, for example.

You’ve delimited your subaccounts from other subaccounts. This step is particularly important when connecting on-premise systems via the cloud connector (see Chapter 5) because, in this case, you can only set up permissions for the connected development systems on the development subaccount.

You’ve specified where the use of identity providers is documented and how many identity providers you want to use.

You’ve ensured that only a restricted group of users can make changes to the production configuration of the identity providers.

The cloud security concept has been coordinated and is coherent with the local security concept. Pay particular attention to which (all?) of your company’s users can log on to the cloud applications.

You’ve ensured that the platform roles match the user roles in other systems on an application-by-application basis. User roles should be aligned.

Table 3.2     Security and Permissions Checklist for SAP BTP, Neo Environment

3.6    Real-World Examples of User and Authorization Management

In this section, we’ll show you how to solve common user management and authorization management tasks in SAP BTP, Neo environment using two concrete real-world use cases. In the first example, you’ll learn how to set up two-factor authentication for administration users in the Identity Authentication service. In this case, this service acts as a platform identity provider. In the second example, you’ll set up Microsoft Azure AD as an application identity provider. From an attribute in the Microsoft Azure AD user master data, you’ll derive a role in SAP BTP that authorizes access to SAP Web IDE.

3.6.1    Two-Factor Authentication of Subaccount Admins Using the Platform Identity Provider

We’ve encountered the scenario described in this section in several projects. Therefore, we want to show you the configuration step by step. A typical requirement in many companies is to implement twofactor or multifactor authentication for certain user groups, such as administrators. In many cases, this security is required because these user groups can access the configuration of critical infrastructure components. This concern is not only true in the on-premise world, but also in the cloud and thus in SAP BTP.

This requirement cannot be solved with the SAP ID service in its role as platform identity provider. You must use the Identity Authentication service as the platform identity provider. (We described its configuration in Chapter 2, Section 2.4.1.) At this point, we’ll show you the steps required to enable two-factor authentication.

We assume that you’ve already set up the Identity Authentication service as an identity provider. For this task, you must enable the SAP Platform Identity Provider service for SAP BTP, which is disabled by default. You must also enable the Identity Authentication tenant as a platform identity provider in the trust management of the subaccount.

Let’s start by configuring two-factor authentication in the Identity Authentication service by following these steps:

  1. First, navigate to Trust Management in the subaccount.

  2. Open the Platform Identity Provider tab and click the Administration Console button.

  3. Log on to the Identity Authentication service with an administration user and navigate to Applications & Resources • Applications.

  4. Search for the appropriate application based on the technical name of the subaccount. If you get two matches, as in Figure 3.56, the service is used both as an application identity provider and platform identity provider. The application whose name is composed of SAP Cloud Platform and the technical subaccount name corresponds to the platform identity provider.

    Figure 3.56     Applications for Subaccount Identity Providers

  5. The configuration of the application identity provider and the platform identity provider are performed independently. First, click on the application of the platform identity provider.

  6. In the application details, switch to the Authentication and Access tab and click on Risk-Based Authentication, as shown in Figure 3.57.

  7. Any number of authentication rules may exist, and one default rule will appear. The default rule is applied if no other rule applies. In our example shown in Figure 3.58, the Allow action is set for the default rule, which means that no further restrictions apply and additional authentication is not required for all users to whom this rule applies.

    Figure 3.57     Invoking Settings for Risk-Based Authentication

    Figure 3.58     Creating Authentication Rules

  8. Click the Add Rule button to create a new rule.

  9. In the creation dialog box, we’ll set up a time-based one-time password (TOTP) for two-factor authentication. Select the TOTP Two Factor Authentication option in the Action dropdown list. In our example, we want this rule to apply to all users assigned to Identity Authentication of a specific user group.

  10. Select the Cloud Group option and open the input help in the field below, as shown in Figure 3.59.

    Figure 3.59     Configuring Risk-Based Authentication

  11. In the dialog box that opens, select the SubAccount_Admin group, as shown in Figure 3.60. This user group was created in preparation for this exercise. (You learned how to create user groups earlier in Section 3.4.2.)

    Figure 3.60     Selecting a User Group

  12. Finally, confirm your settings by clicking OK.

The authentication rule will now be added to the rule hierarchy, as shown in Figure 3.61. If you’ve defined multiple rules, click the arrow buttons in the Priority column to change the prioritization of these rules. The rule discovery process is aborted as soon as a matching rule is found. The rules are processed from top to bottom; the rule at the top of the list is applied first, and the rule at the bottom of the list is applied last.

Figure 3.61     Authentication Rules

[»]  Additional Authentication Rules

Risk-based authentication enables a great deal of flexibility. The configuration we described can be done in a similar way for the application identity provider.

To enable you to test the configuration, assign the SubAccount_Admin group to your user. For this task, navigate to the user’s detail view, select User Groups, and then click the Assign Groups button, as shown in Figure 3.62.

Figure 3.62     Assigning User Groups

Select the user group that is typically used and then click the Save button, as shown in Figure 3.63.

Figure 3.63     Selecting a User Group

Now, let’s assign the subaccount user. For this task, switch back to the SAP BTP cockpit of the subaccount. Navigate to Member Management and add the user IDs from the Identity Authentication service to the list of users. Make sure you’ve selected the correct User Base, as shown in Figure 3.64.

Figure 3.64     Assigning Users to a Subaccount

[»]  Duplicate User Maintenance in the Identity Authentication Service and Subaccount

The Identity Authentication service is responsible for authenticating users in the role of SAML identity provider. The service manages all users centrally and across service providers. However, the service provider cannot access this user base. It only receives a SAML ticket in which user identities and group memberships in the Identity Authentication service are passed as information. Therefore, the users must be created in advance in the service provider, as well as in the platform identity provider, to assign them the appropriate platform roles.

In the next step, let’s test our configuration of two-factor authentication. For this task, in Trust Management , under the Platform Identity Provider tab, click the Cockpit button. You’ll be prompted to authenticate with user credentials from the Identity Authentication service user base, as shown in Figure 3.65.

Figure 3.65     Logging on with an Identity Authentication User

After a user has been successfully authenticated, the Identity Authentication service forwards the user data for two-factor authentication. As we’ve configured, the TOTP method is used for this. In other words, the administrator who wants to log on must read the displayed QR code with an authenticator app and enter the displayed security code in the logon screen, as shown in Figure 3.66. Different apps can be used for authentication. For our example, we chose Microsoft Authenticator. In principle, SAP Authenticator and other apps would also work.

Figure 3.66     Two-Factor Authentication

After the security code has been successfully verified, the administrator is taken to the global account. Now, the administrator can jump to a subaccount.

3.6.2    Authenticating SAP Web IDE Users via Microsoft Azure AD

In the next scenario, we’ll look at how a third-party or external identity provider can be used as an application identity provider directly from the subaccount. With Microsoft Azure AD in the role of SAML 2.0 identity provider, you can use a common tool. The aim of our sample configuration is to enable developers to authenticate themselves against Microsoft Azure AD to use SAP Web IDE. Alternatively, you could use the Identity Authentication service as a proxy to the third-party identity provider.

First, you should check whether SAP Web IDE has already been activated in your subaccount. For this task, navigate to the Services area in the subaccount and search for “Web IDE.” If the status Enabled is not displayed on its tile, as shown in Figure 3.67, you must jump to the details screen by clicking on the tile and then activate the service.

Figure 3.67     Checking the Activation Status of SAP Web IDE

As explained earlier in Section 3.3, in SAML 2.0 scenarios, a trust relationship must be established between the service provider and the identity provider. In our case, the service provider is the SAP BTP subaccount. The mutual import of metadata is required to establish the trust relationship, which can be achieved by following these steps:

  1. In the subaccount, navigate to Trust Management in the Security menu area.

  2. Under the Local Service Provider tab, check that the Custom configuration type has been selected and that Principal Propagation has been enabled (Enabled).

  3. Click the Get Metadata button to start downloading the metadata in the form of an XML file, as shown in Figure 3.68.

Figure 3.68     Downloading the Metadata for the Service Provider

Now, we’re ready to configure Microsoft Azure AD:

  1. Log on to https://portal.azure.com on the Microsoft Azure platform, as shown in Figure 3.69. You can also implement this scenario with a trial account.

  2. On the homepage, in the Azure services section, select Azure AD Domain…, as shown in Figure 3.70.

    Figure 3.69     Logging on to Microsoft Azure

    Figure 3.70     Microsoft Azure AD

  3. In the side menu, select the Enterprise applications option, as shown in Figure 3.71. On this overview screen, you’ll configure the necessary service providers.

    Figure 3.71     Jumping to Enterprise Applications

  4. You must now create a new application. Click on the + New application button, as shown in Figure 3.72.

    Figure 3.72     Creating a New Application

  5. As shown in Figure 3.73, more than 3,700 predefined systems can be registered as service providers. Search for the term “SAP.” From the list of results, click on SAP Cloud Platform.

    Figure 3.73     Adding SAP BTP as a Service Provider

  6. In the dialog box on the right, confirm the creation of the service provider by clicking the Add button, as shown in Figure 3.74.

    At this point, you have the option of customizing the name of the service provider. We recommend that you use descriptive names.

    Figure 3.74     Confirming a Service Provider

  7. A few seconds is required for this process to complete. The progress is displayed in the upper-right pane, as shown in Figure 3.75.

    Figure 3.75     Application Status

  8. After the service provider has been successfully created, you can jump to the details to start the configuration of SSO by clicking on the Set up single sign on tile, as shown in Figure 3.76.

  9. In the next step, the SSO method must be selected. We’ll use the SAML option for this example, as shown in Figure 3.77.

    Figure 3.76     Setting Up SSO

    Figure 3.77     Selecting SAML as the SSO Method

  10. Now, upload the metadata of the service provider by clicking Upload metadata file, as shown in Figure 3.78.

    Figure 3.78     Uploading the Metadata of the Service Provider

  11. In the following dialog box, select the metadata file previously exported from the subaccount.

  12. Basic information for the connection is already included in the metadata document. You only need to maintain the Sign on URL parameter, as shown in Figure 3.79. In our example, we used the SAP Web IDE endpoint.

    Figure 3.79     Maintaining the Logon URL for the SAP BTP Service

  13. Finally, click on the Save button. This step will take you back to the service provider details.

  14. Now, we’ll adapt the SAML attributes to our requirements. For this task, click Edit in the Attributes & Claims area, as shown in Figure 3.80.

    Figure 3.80     Editing SAML Attributes

  15. Customize the user identifier used in the SAML assertion. By default, the userprincipalname attribute is used. However, for our purposes, we want to use the user’s email address. For this task, click the Unique User Identifier entry in the Required claim area, as shown in Figure 3.81.

    Figure 3.81     Customizing a User Identifier

  16. In the Source attribute dropdown list, select user.mail and save the change, as shown in Figure 3.82.

    Figure 3.82     Changing a Source Attribute

  17. In this scenario, we’ll query the user’s department to determine whether the user is allowed to start SAP Web IDE. For this task, include the Department attribute as a claim in the SAML assertion. Click Add new claim in the Attributes & Claims area, as shown in Figure 3.83.

    Figure 3.83     Adding a New Claim

  18. In the Name field, enter “Department” for department and select user.department in the Source attribute dropdown list, as shown in Figure 3.84. This step will specify that the value of the user.department attribute will be copied to the claim named Department.

    Figure 3.84     Creating a Claim

  19. Save the claim.

  20. On the service provider overview screen, navigate to the SAML Signing Certificate section. Now, in the Federation Metadata XML row, click the Download link to download the metadata, as shown in Figure 3.85.

Figure 3.85     Downloading the Service Provider Metadata

You now must add the desired user(s) to Microsoft Azure AD as the service provider. For this task, click on the Users and Groups menu item in Microsoft Azure AD. Then, click Add User/Group and select the desired user.

As a final step, you must configure Microsoft Azure AD as an application identity provider in the SAP BTP subaccount. For this task, navigate to Trust Management as usual, open the Application Identity Provider tab, and click the Add Trusted Identity Provider link, as shown in Figure 3.86.

Next, import the Microsoft Azure AD metadata. For this task, click the Browse button. Select the XML file that was previously exported from Microsoft Azure AD, as shown in Figure 3.87.

Figure 3.86     Adding a Trusted Identity Provider

Figure 3.87     Importing Trusted Identity Provider Metadata

You’ll now need to make some adjustments since some values are not prepopulated by Microsoft Azure. Change the value of Assertion Consumer Service dropdown list to Assertion Consumer Service to correctly capture SAP BTP as a service. In the Signature Algorithm dropdown list, select SHA-256, which may be the current default value. Verify that the Enabled checkbox has been selected, as shown in Figure 3.88.

Figure 3.88     Setting the Trusted Identity Provider

Next, you’ll need to map the attributes of the SAML assertion to what are called principal attributes (i.e., the user attributes in SAP BTP). The term “principal” in this context refers to the user and is the term used in the SAML specification. For this task, switch to the Attributes tab and click the Add Assertion-Based Attribute link, as shown in Figure 3.89. Maintain the values as listed in Table 3.3.

Figure 3.89     Adopting Assertion Attributes

Assertion Attributes

Principal Attributes

http://schemas.xmlsoap.org/ws/2005/05/identity/claims/givenname

firstName

http://schemas.xmlsoap.org/ws/2005/05/identity/claims/emailaddress

email

http://schemas.xmlsoap.org/ws/2005/05/identity/claims/surname

lastName

Table 3.3     Mapping the SAML Assertion Attributes

As mentioned earlier, we’ll derive the authorization to start SAP Web IDE from the employee’s department. Under the Groups tab, click the Add Assertion-Based Group link to define an appropriate user group. In the Group field, select the SAP_BTP_Security_Book group that we created in Section 3.4.2. In the Mapping Rules field, specify the value “Department,” then select the equals operation in the next dropdown list, and enter the value “Development” for the development department, as shown in Figure 3.90. Save the configuration of the identity provider.

Figure 3.90     Deriving User Groups from Assertion Attributes

Now, set the identity provider we just created as the default identity provider. For this task, under the Application Identity Provider tab, click the radio button in the Default column in the corresponding row, as shown in Figure 3.91.

Figure 3.91     Selecting Microsoft Azure AD as the Default Identity Provider

Now, add a role with permissions to start SAP Web IDE to the SAP_BTP_Security_Book group. For this task, navigate to the user group maintenance as usual and select the group in the list. Then, click the Assign button in the Roles area, as shown in Figure 3.92.

Figure 3.92     Assigning a Role in Group Maintenance

In the role assignment dialog box, select the sapwebide EU2 subaccount, the di application, and the DiDeveloper role, as shown in Figure 3.93. This role is predefined to obtain developer rights for the SAP Web IDE application. Save your assignment.

Figure 3.93     Selecting the DiDeveloper Role

You can now test the configuration of the identity provider by calling SAP Web IDE. For this task, jump down to SAP Web IDE in the service overview and click the Go to Service link, as shown in Figure 3.94.

Figure 3.94     Launching SAP Web IDE

You’ll be redirected to Microsoft Azure AD and will need to authenticate with your Microsoft credentials, as shown in Figure 3.95. If authentication does not work, check that the development department has been correctly maintained for your user in the Microsoft Azure AD user master.

Figure 3.95     Logging on Using Microsoft Azure AD as the Identity Provider

In this chapter, we’ve provided a detailed introduction to security and permissions in SAP BTP, Neo environment. This information should enable you to competently and efficiently solve common requirements, from conception to implementation to the final configuration of security and permissions. Based on our examples, we hope you see how easy implementing two-factor authentication can be. Even the use of a third-party identity provider isn’t difficult and can be implemented with a few targeted clicks, as shown in the second example.

3.7    Summary

In this chapter, you learned about configuring security and authorizations in SAP BTP, Neo environment. Starting with the command-line setup, we then walked you through key settings for user administration. We then discussed setting up identity providers via trust configuration and explained how to manage authorizations by creating and managing roles and user groups. We provided a handy checklist you can consult when managing security and permissions in SAP BTP, Neo environment, and finally, we walked you through some real-world examples showcasing two-factor authentication and Microsoft Azure AD.

4    Configuring Security and Authorizations in the Cloud Foundry Environment

Having covered security concepts and their implementation in SAP Business Technology Platform (SAP BTP), Neo environment in the previous chapter, we’ll now turn to the specifics of SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment in this chapter. We’ll cover numerous security options in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment in detail.

In this chapter, we’ll look at SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment and its security aspects. You’ll learn everything important about roles and authorizations in this environment as well as about mappings, for example, with users of external identity providers.

In Section 4.1, we’ll introduce you to the command-line tool as an alternative operating option for SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. In Section 4.2, you’ll learn all about user management in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, including how to assign basic permissions. In Section 4.3, we’ll show you how to configure a trust relationship between identity providers and explore the technical foundation behind trust.

In Section 4.4, we’ll go into the management of permissions. You’ll learn about the different types of roles and options for dynamic mappings between authorizations and user attributes. Finally, we’ll explore some reporting options available for permissions.

In Section 4.5, we’ve provided checklist that you can use to verify that you’ve made all the important security settings. We’ll conclude this chapter in Section 4.6 with two practical examples: one for secure service configuration and another for Microsoft Azure Active Directory (Microsoft Azure AD) integration.

[»]  Versions of SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment

Currently, two different versions of SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment exist, called feature sets. Feature set A and feature set B differ in the design of their interfaces and their features, among other things.

SAP has announced that all customers will be migrated to feature set B in several phases. Therefore, in this book, we’ll only cover the interface activated by feature set B.

In the SAP BTP cockpit, you can determine whether your account is running feature set B by selecting the About option in your personal menu, as shown in Figure 4.1.

Figure 4.1     Calling Up the System Information in the User Menu

Under Cloud Management Tools, you’ll find your feature set specification, as shown in Figure 4.2.

Figure 4.2     Locating Feature Set Information in the About Dialog Box

4.1    Setting Up the Command Line for SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment

Administration tasks for SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment can be performed using a console, called the Cloud Foundry command-line interface (CLI ), just like in SAP BTP, Neo environment. Since the Cloud Foundry CLI was not developed by SAP, the download is not offered by SAP, but via a public Git repository. An operating system-specific installation file and instructions can be found at https://github.com/cloudfoundry/cli#downloads. If you download the relevant binary version, an installation program is included for which no further prior knowledge is required.

With the following command, you can check whether the console is working. If so, the help command will display a list of all available commands:

cf help

With the following command, you can display all commands available in the console:

cf help -a

If you want to see all the options for a particular command, include the help command. In the following example, the result would be the options available with the login command:

cf help login

To run commands from the console in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, the first step is to log on using the command shown in Listing 4.1. At a minimum, you must pass the application programming interface (API) endpoint for your service instance, your organization, and your user ID.

cf login 
      -a <yourApiEndpoint>
      -o <yourOrganization>
      -u <yourEmailAddress>

Listing 4.1     SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment: Logging on with the login Command

You can determine the values for the required parameters using the SAP BTP cockpit. For this task, navigate to the overview for your subaccount. You can find the name of your organization and the API endpoint in the Cloud Foundry Environment section, in the Org Name and API Endpoint fields, as shown in Figure 4.3.

Figure 4.3     SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment: Logon Parameters

The Cloud Foundry CLI can be used not only to administer SAP BTP, but also for all other Cloud Foundry implementations. This tool can be extended through plug-ins on a vendor-specific basis. SAP has also made use of this extensibility by delivering the following plug-ins:

  • Plug-in for multitarget applications (MTAs) for distributed applications with a common lifecycle

  • Plug-in service factory to support backups and restores of service instances

  • Plug-in for PostgreSQL database migrations

  • Plug-in for custom domains to support custom domains in applications

You can find these plug-ins at https://tools.hana.ondemand.com/#cloud, as shown in Figure 4.4.

Use the following command to install a plug-in in the Cloud Foundry CLI:

cf install-plugin <yourPath>/custom-domain-cli

The name of the plug-in is composed of the path (i.e., darwin-amd64) and the name of the plug-in (i.e., custom-domain-cli). The path is derived from the environment (Linux, Windows, or macOS) for which you have downloaded the plug-in. The path refers to the directory that was created on your computer after unpacking the archive.

Figure 4.4     SAP Plug-Ins for the Cloud Foundry CLI

Use the following command to view the plug-ins installed in your Cloud Foundry CLI:

cf plugins

You’ll learn about another command-line tool for managing your SAP BTP global account in Chapter 6, Section 6.2.

4.2    User Management

You can add users as members to an organization within your subaccount in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. In turn, you’ll assign roles to these members to authorize them to perform actions. A prerequisite for creating members and assigning roles is that you have been assigned the organization manager role. The user who created the subaccount is automatically added as a member and assigned this role. For more information on organizations and dependencies in subaccounts, see Chapter 1, Section 1.3.3.

You can also add members to a space and assign roles to these members. The prerequisite for this capability is that you must have the space manager or organization manager role. If you only have the space manager role, the users you want to add to the space as members must have already been assigned to the organization as members beforehand. If you have the organization manager role, the new member will not only be added to the space, but also to the parent organization automatically. In SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, members are identified at the subaccount level and the space level solely by their email addresses.

The user administration screen can be accessed via the menu path Security • Users in the SAP BTP cockpit. Figure 4.5 shows a standard view of the user management screen for an administration user that has been assigned as an identity provider via the SAP ID service. In our example, another user that has been provided via Microsoft Azure AD. For both users, the email address is specified as the User Name in each case.

Figure 4.5     Overview of Users in the Subaccount

Click the Create button to create new users. For users originating from the SAP ID service (as indicated by Default identity provider in the overview screen), you should create users explicitly (for more information, see Section 4.3.1). For other identity providers, you do not need to add users explicitly.

On the user creation screen, enter the user’s email address in the User Name and E-Mail fields as a unique characteristic, as shown in Figure 4.6. Also specify the Identity Provider that provides this user. Confirm your entries by clicking the Create button.

Figure 4.6     Creating a User

After creating the user, to show the user detail view, you can check the user data again by clicking the arrow on the right side of the line , as shown in Figure 4.7. At this point, you can also directly assign roles to the user, which we describe in Section 4.4.1. You can also delete a user by clicking the Delete button.

Figure 4.7     Showing User Details

For administrators, this process is the default way to create new users at the subaccount level. On the other hand, if you want end users to have access to a cloud application running in a subaccount of SAP BTP, users are usually created automatically from the company’s own identity provider. For this task, you’ll select a checkmark during identity provider configuration (see Section 4.3.2 and Section 4.3.3). In practice, this setting saves you several steps when creating users for end users.

[»]  Administration Users

A point worth mentioning is that you should have at least two administrators assigned the subaccount-specific standard subaccount admin role. This approach allows you to manage the subaccount comprehensively. You should also manage at least two users via the default identity provider in case the external identity provider is ever unavailable.

4.3    Trust Configuration

To configure trust relationships, you should be familiar with the basics of authentication, as described in Chapter 2, Section 2.2. Specifically, be sure you understand how identity providers and subaccounts interact, as described in Chapter 2, Section 2.2.1.

You can find the administration screen for trusted identity providers in the SAP BTP cockpit under Security • Trust Configuration, as shown in Figure 4.8.

In this overview screen, you’ll see all providers currently configured. By default, the Default identity provider is activated, which we describe in Section 4.3.1. Alternatively, you can set up a platform identity provider; more on this topic in Section 4.3.2. To use your own provider, you can set up a custom identity provider, which we cover in Section 4.3.3. A trust relationship must be explicitly set up for each additional provider.

Figure 4.8     SAP BTP Cockpit: Trust Configuration

[»]  Comparison of SAP BTP, Neo Environment and SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment

If you have already worked through Chapter 3, you’ll notice some differences between the environments when reading the following sections. See Chapter 2, Section 2.2.1, for notes on the differences between SAP BTP, Neo environment and SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment.

4.3.1    Setting Up the SAP ID Service

The SAP ID service serves as the default identity provider. On the screen, this service is referred to as the Default identity provider or with the value sap.default in the Origin Key field. This identity provider cannot be deleted but can be configured. To configure it, click the pencil icon in the Actions column to switch to change mode. If you’re sure that your platform identity provider and/or third-party identity provider correctly authenticates all users, you can disable SAP ID service as an identity provider by deselecting the Available for User Logon checkbox, as shown in Figure 4.9.

Select the Create Shadow Users During Logon option to control whether users listed in the default identity provider are automatically created in your SAP BTP tenant. This option is quite useful for your own identity providers. In the context of the SAP ID service, however, selecting this checkbox means that all users who have an account in the SAP ID service can log on as users of your cloud applications. They only need to know the link required to call the application. Therefore, this setting is inactive by default for SAP ID service, and we do not recommend enabling it for this identity provider.

Figure 4.9     Disabling the SAP ID Service as the Identity Provider

4.3.2    Setting Up a Platform Identity Provider

Using a platform identity provider is another option in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. The name of the provider comes from the fact that the service runs on SAP BTP for authentication. The Identity Authentication service is used, which is run in a separate tenant. The use of this service is already included in the license for some SAP products (in a bundled license). The main task of Identity Authentication is to serve as a central identity provider for all subaccounts.

To connect an Identity Authentication tenant, click the Establish Trust button, as shown in Figure 4.10. Setup is easier via this route than with a customer-owned identity provider but follows the same process in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment as in SAP BTP, Neo environment (see Chapter 3, Section 3.3.2).

If the Identity Authentication tenant is not in the same subaccount, you can use the New Trust Configuration button to establish a new trust relationship with the service. In this case, you must download the Security Assertion Markup Language (SAML) metadata yourself and upload it to the Identity Authentication tenant. Downloading the metadata follows the same process as described for the application identity provider in Chapter 3, Section 3.3.3. To upload the metadata, use the Identity Authentication tenant’s administration console, which you can access at https://<tenant>.accounts.ondemand.com/admin, where you’ll find SAML 2.0 Configuration in the Configuration section. Under Define from Metadata, you can upload the XML file from SAP BTP.

Figure 4.10     Connecting the Identity Authentication Service to SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment

4.3.3    Setting Up Third-Party Identity Providers

The external identity providers used are primarily central SAML 2.0-capable services. You need these external identity providers to serve as a basis for extended services for user account and authentication (XSUAA)-based composite role mappings, a concept we’ll explain in detail in Section 4.4.3.

You can start setting up the trust relationship (based on SAML) by downloading the metadata of the service provider (i.e., SAP BTP). For this task, navigate to Security • Trust Configuration and click the SAML Metadata button, as shown in Figure 4.10. You can then upload the resulting metadata file in XML format to the target application (i.e., the external identity provider).

Once the metadata has been imported into your identity provider, upload the provider’s SAML response in the trust configuration of the SAP BTP cockpit by clicking the New Trust Configuration button. The popup window shown in Figure 4.11 opens.

Figure 4.11     Create SAML Trust Configuration

For these settings, follow these steps:

  1. In the Metadata field, click the Upload button and upload the XML file containing the identity provider response. During this step, the Origin Key field is usually prepopulated directly. This key uniquely identifies the identity provider.

  2. In the Name field, enter an internal name for the identity provider, which you can choose freely.

  3. Select the Available for User Logon checkbox to enable externally managed users to log on.

  4. If you select the Create Shadow Users During Logon checkbox, a user is automatically created in the user management environment in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment when an externally managed user logs on for the first time. This option thus eliminates the need to explicitly create users in the subaccount. Both checkboxes are active by default.

  5. In the Link Text for User Logon field, enter a display text for the link that will be shown to users on the logon page for the subaccount. Clicking on this link will take users to the linked identity provider.

  6. Save your settings by clicking the Save button.

In Section 4.6.2, we’ll walk you through the complete process of setting up an external identity provider using Microsoft Azure AD as an example.

4.4    Authorization Management

SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment has a comprehensive permission management functionality. Roles can be grouped into composite roles and assigned to users or user groups. You can also assign roles to users with similar other attributes. In this section, we’ll show you how to handle both standard roles and composite roles.

4.4.1    Creating and Managing Roles

The available standard roles are listed in the SAP BTP cockpit via the menu path Security • Roles, as shown in Figure 4.12.

Figure 4.12     SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment: Roles

The overview screen contains the following columns:

  • Application Name
    This column contains the technical name of the application that contains the role template.

  • Application Description
    This column contains the description of the application if a description has been maintained by the development teams.

  • Role Template
    Under Role Template, you can see the identification of the role assigned by the development team.

  • Role Name
    In this field, you can find the name of the role template in the form of a link. By clicking on this name, you can jump to the assignments of the role template and edit them.

  • Role Description
    In this column, you’ll find additional explanations deposited by the developer teams to the role concerned.

Using the control fields in the last two columns, you can create customer-specific roles based on the standard roles as templates (Add Using Same Role Template), or you can delete roles .

[»]  Role Template Development

Role templates are created by SAP BTP application development teams and are usually hard coded into applications. As a result, if you need additional role templates, development teams would need to customize the respective applications. For this task, you can use the Add Using Same Role Template function.

Applications are also available to help you create new roles on the fly. A prominent example is the SAP Launchpad service, which creates a new role each time a new tile is created in the SAP Fiori launchpad to enable easy website access. For a demonstration of this functionality, refer to Section 4.6.1.

Now, for any role template, click the link in the Role Name column, for example, for the Subaccount Viewer role. You’ll get to the Role: <Role Name> - Overview view shown in Figure 4.13.

All the information on this page except for the assigned Role Collections (the composite roles) comes from the application for which the role was developed.

You can edit the composite roles assigned to the role template by clicking on the Edit button in the upper right. In edit mode, as shown in Figure 4.14, you can add the role to other composite roles by clicking the plus sign icon . You can also remove a role from a composite roles again by clicking on the trash icon .

Figure 4.13     Overview of a Role Template

Figure 4.14     Adding and Deleting a Composite Role Assignment

The ability to jump directly to editing a composite role by clicking the arrow icon at the end of a line is particularly practical. On this screen, you can also check which other roles have already been assigned to the composite role.

Another way to find role templates for applications is to select Instances and Subscriptions from the SAP BTP cockpit menu. On the Instances and Subscriptions overview screen, you can determine which roles belong to an application by clicking on the three dots icon at the end of a line, as shown in Figure 4.15. In the action menu, select the Manage Roles function.

Figure 4.15     Overview of Instances and Applications

Selecting this function will take you to the detail page for the application instance, as shown in Figure 4.16. On the right, you’ll see all the role templates contained in this application instance. Starting from this overview screen, you can add new composite roles by clicking the plus icon , or you can edit existing roles by clicking the pencil icon .

Using this view is especially useful for finding out everyone has access to a particular application. For example, if the Role Collections and Attributes columns display a value of “0,” at this point, you can assume that no one can use the application in question (assuming the application was programmed correctly).

Figure 4.16     Finding and Editing Roles via the Application Overview

4.4.2    Maintaining Composite Roles

As mentioned earlier, composite roles in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment are called role collections. Composite roles are formed from individual roles and assigned to users or groups of users.

You can maintain composite roles via the menu path Security • Role Collections, as shown in Figure 4.17.

Before assigning composite roles to users, a best practice is to create your own composite roles as copies of existing templates. We do not recommend changing the roles provided in the SAP subaccount area. If necessary, you can rebuild these standard roles.

For SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, SAP delivers the default role collections listed in Table 4.1.

Figure 4.17     Getting Started with Role Collections Maintenance

Role

Description

SAP Business Application Studio administrator

This role is intended for the administration of SAP Business Application Studio. This role can also export and delete user data.

SAP Business Application Studio developer

This role is intended for developers working in SAP Business Application Studio.

SAP Business Application Studio extension deployer

This role allows the deployment of applications from SAP Business Application Studio.

Cloud connector administrator

This role enables the administration of the cloud connector.

Connectivity and destination administrator

This role enables the maintenance of cloud connector connections and their corresponding destinations in the SAP BTP cockpit.

Destination administrator

This role only allows the maintenance of destinations, not the administration of the cloud connector.

Subaccount administrator

This role is the most important role collection delivered by SAP because it allows you to comprehensively manage your subaccount. Ideally, this role is always assigned to at least two people.

Subaccount service administrator

This role can be used to manage the services of a subaccount.

Subaccount viewer

This role allows read access to a subaccount, for example, to check role assignments.

Table 4.1     Composite Roles Delivered by SAP

To create a new composite role, follow these steps:

  1. In the Role Collections area, click the plus icon to the right, above the list of composite roles.

  2. In the popup window, maintain the Name and Description fields for the new role collection, as shown in Figure 4.18, and save your entries.
