In the Authentication field, you can configure the authentication of users at the connection level, for example, if a Security Assertion Markup Language (SAML) ticket or another type of authentication is to be used to communicate with the connection.

Finally, you can store a location in the Location ID field. If used, this specification must match the IDs used when configuring the cloud connector (as shown earlier in Figure 5.7). By default, therefore, this field remains empty.

If the target is an SAP system, specify the property sap-client to the desired client. For this task, click on the New Property button. You’ll be offered a list of properties that can be set optionally. In practice, only the sap-client setting is relevant. If further settings are required, these settings can be found in the documentation of the cloud application that is to be connected.

The situation is similar for RFC connections. In this case, too, the virtual host name is used. If metadata and application data should be transmitted separately (i.e., for example, the metadata is queried via a function module with a dedicated user), a distinction can be made between User and Repository User on the creation screen, as shown in Figure 5.37. The optional Repository User (with its corresponding Repository Password) is used to query the metadata, and the User (with its Password) is used for all other data, for instance, the specific function module calls.

Figure 5.37     Creating an RFC Destination

For the connection data, all properties of the JCo can be maintained in the properties. This connector is the only way to directly access remote-enabled function modules of an ABAP system. This capability works similarly to an HTTP(s) connection. For example, when connecting via the message server, select the property jco.client.mshost, and when connecting via the application server, select jco.client.ashost.

After creating and saving a destination, you can test the connection by clicking the Check Connection button. If problems with the certificates for HTTP(S) connections exist, you can find possible solutions in Chapter 2, Section 2.1.1.

If a connection can be established successfully, configuring the permissions on both the subscription level and on the application level make sense. This procedure is described in Chapter 2, Section 2.1.2.

Connections to SAP Web IDE are a special case. If the cloud connector is to be used to connect an application developed in SAP Web IDE, the extended settings of the connection, under Additional Properties, must be maintained. As shown in Figure 5.38, for example, the following properties of the connection have been configured:

  • WebIDEEnabled = true enables the connection for SAP Web IDE.

  • WebIDESystem = gateway controls access to SAP Gateway.

  • WebIDEUsage = odata_gen, odata_abap, bsp_execute_abap denotes the executable actions.

Figure 5.38     Additional Parameters for Connections to SAP Web IDE

5.5.2    Creating Destinations in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment

To create a destination in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, navigate to Connectivity • Destinations in your subaccount. Click on the New Destination button. On the creation screen, enter the virtual host name in the URL field to set up a connection via the cloud connector, as shown in Figure 5.39. Choosing the On-Premise option in the Proxy Type field makes it clear that the connection is to be routed via a cloud connector.

If you develop a cloud application in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, in our example, the abstract name ERP-DEV can now be used for the destination to resolve the corresponding URI.

Figure 5.39     SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment: Creating a Destination

To secure the connections and for any certificate issues, refer to Chapter 2, Section 2.1.1.

5.6    Checklist for Cloud Connector Configuration

Table 5.1 provides a checklist for administering secure connections with the help of SAP’s cloud connector.

Completed

Task

You’ve specified that the cloud connector is mandatory when developing cloud services and applications that use an on-premise backend system. Ideally, this requirement has been added to your development guidelines.

You’ve defined how many cloud connector instances will be installed, distributed geographically to optimize failsafe. Also consider whether developers should have local cloud connector instances running on their devices.

You’ve developed a concept for the maintenance of your cloud connector instances. You should determine which cloud connector instance will be upgraded first in response to feature upgrades, and you should ensure that the connections are tested correctly.

You’ve defined who should have access to the cloud connector. Some possibilities are administrators and developers, but also other groups of users might need viewing rights.

Depending on who is getting access, you should have clarified whether you want to use a connection to an LDAP directory to authenticate users. Such a connection makes sense in many cases, especially if several users need access.

A monitoring concept for the cloud connector has been developed. As a guide, you can use concepts that are already available for monitoring other system connections in your company. Make sure you also record who is responsible for monitoring.

You’ve defined a location for documenting cloud connector connections. In this context, use your development guidelines to check whether documentation within cloud connector is sufficient or determine what other documentation will be affected. An important step is to record the subaccounts and the connected cloud connector instances.

If data protection requirements apply to the data transmitted via the cloud connector, we advise following the four-eyes principle when monitoring network traces.

You’ve reached agreement on how high the availability of the connection should be. SAP generally recommends a redundant, high-availability connection. Also consider your own internet uplink and work out a fallback scenario. This scenario usually requires extensive clarification with network teams.

You’ve developed a naming convention for virtual host names and destinations. The naming rule can be technical (e.g., use “FI” for “finance”) or application specific (e.g., use “MTE” for “mobile travel expenses” applications) but should be consistent throughout.

You’ve defined processes for the activation of services and documented which people must be informed by which means.

Table 5.1     Checklist for Configuring the Cloud Connector

5.7    Real-World Examples of Secure Cloud Connector Configuration

Scenarios for using the cloud connector are diverse. In this section, we’ve put together some typical use cases for you: connecting to a local Git server and accessing an SAP S/4HANA system. You’ll also learn how to configure LDAP authentication.

5.7.1    Connecting an On-Premise Git Server to SAP BTP, Neo Environment

Git is a free software product that enables version control for the source files of your development projects. The open-source software was invented by Linus Torvalds, creator of Linux. In this section, we’ll show you how to connect a Git server to SAP BTP, Neo environment. We chose this scenario for our first real-life example because we realized that few companies care about how the source files are managed when using SAP Web IDE. However, this consideration should be a central issue since you can save these files in SAP Web IDE, but you must export them manually and import them again whenever another developer wants to work on the same application. When deploying an SAP Fiori application to an ABAP repository, only the compiled application files are transferred, not the source files. We therefore recommend that you actually carry out the following steps for connecting a Git repository in your company.

In this section, we assume that your development team consists of several people who develop cloud applications on SAP Web IDE. Within your on-premise system landscape, you already have a Git server for managing all the source files of these developments.

The following preconditions must be met:

  • You must know the credentials for your Git server.

  • The cloud connector must have been installed and configured as described earlier in Section 5.2.

  • Ideally, you should already have access to SAP Web IDE.

First, you’ll configure the cloud connector so that it allows a connection to your Git repository. Keep in mind that Git is not an SAP system.

For this configuration task, follow these steps:

  1. Within the administration console of the cloud connector, first select the SAP BTP subaccount on which SAP Web IDE is running, as shown in Figure 5.40.

    Figure 5.40     Cloud Connector Administration: Subaccount Overview

  2. Select the Cloud To On-Premise menu item. Under the ACCESS CONTROL tab, click the plus icon to create a new system mapping.

  3. Select the system type option Non-SAP System from the Back-end Type dropdown list, as shown in Figure 5.41.

    Figure 5.41     Selecting the System Type for Non-SAP Systems

  4. Depending on the server, select the connection type HTTP or HTTPS. We recommend you choose the more secure HTTPS connection option.

  5. In the next popup window, select the Git server using its internal host name within your company network, as shown in Figure 5.42.

    Figure 5.42     Git Server Selection

  6. On the next page, you can create a virtual name for the server. In our example, we chose the name “virtualgitserver:8443.” In productive environments, the use of a virtual name makes sense.

  7. In the next step, leave the setting None in the Principal Type field, as shown in Figure 5.43. In this step, you could configure the use of certificate-based authentication.

    Figure 5.43     Selecting the Principal Type

  8. In the last step, confirm your settings by clicking Finish.

The result is an entry in the ACL, under the ACCESS CONTROL tab, that allows access to the Git server. You can test the access by clicking the Check availability of the internal host icon , as shown in Figure 5.44.

Figure 5.44     Testing of the System Connection

Now, let’s unlock all paths, which is performed in the Resources Of <xxx> table. First, enable all subpaths via the / entry by clicking on the plus icon . The result is shown in Figure 5.45. This process allows a connection to all directory paths on the Git server.

Figure 5.45     Unlocking Resource Paths

Next, we’ll need a destination to the Git server:

  1. Log on to your subaccount in SAP BTP, Neo environment.

  2. Navigate to Connectivity • Destinations.

  3. Click on New Destination.

  4. In the Name field, select the virtual name that you have assigned to the Git server in the cloud connector, as shown in Figure 5.46. In the URL field, enter the URI of the Git server. You can leave the default settings in the other fields.

    Figure 5.46     Creating a Destination in the SAP BTP Cockpit

  5. Click New Property and make the following additional settings:

    • WebIDEEnabled = true

    • HTML5.DynamicDestination = true

  6. Finally, save this destination by clicking the Save button.

The next step is to use the Git integration in SAP Web IDE to create a project that has been cloned from the Git repository to SAP Web IDE. Follow these steps:

  1. Call SAP Web IDE, click on the File icon . Then, navigate to GIT • Clone Repository from the menu, as shown in Figure 5.47.

    Figure 5.47     Copying the Git Repository to SAP Web IDE

  2. Enter the virtual server in the URL field and the correct Git repository as the end of the URI (in our example, http://virtualgitserver:8443/Repo.git).

In the cloned project, Git operations are now available via the context menu, as shown in Figure 5.48.

Figure 5.48     Git Operations in the Cloned Project

5.7.2    Connecting SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment to an SAP S/4HANA System

Many companies require a connection between an on-premise SAP S/4HANA system to SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, so that data from the on-premise system can be accessed from SAP BTP.

To create this connection, the following prerequisites must first be met:

  • You must have the system data for your SAP S/4HANA system (i.e., servers, ports, etc.).

  • The SAP S/4HANA system must be accessible for your cloud connector.

  • You must have the access data for your subaccount in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. Your subaccount should have been set up in the cloud connector, as described in Section 5.2.

  • Instances for the SAP Destination service and the SAP Connectivity service have been created in the SAP BTP subaccount. This step must be performed in all spaces in which the destination is to be used. You can also perform the activation later.

In the first step, include the SAP S/4HANA server in a cloud-to-on-premise connection via HTTP(S):

  1. In the cloud connector administration console, navigate to Define Subaccount • Cloud To On-Premise, as shown in Figure 5.49.

    Figure 5.49     Cloud To On-Premise View

  2. Now, we’ll create a new system mapping. Select the system type ABAP System, as shown in Figure 5.50.

  3. In the following step, select the connection type HTTP or HTTPS, depending on the requirements of your company.

    Figure 5.50     Selecting the System Type for an SAP S/4HANA Connection

  4. Enter the internal name and port of the SAP S/4HANA system, as shown in Figure 5.51.

    Figure 5.51     Entering the Internal Name and Port

  5. Now, you can assign a virtual name in the next step. For our example, we’ll use the name s4demo.test.sap and port 8000. Click Next.

  6. For the Principal Type field, leave the existing value (None).

  7. Send the internal host name in the HTTP header. For this task, select the Use Internal Host option, as shown in Figure 5.52.

    Figure 5.52     Passing the Internal Host Name in the HTTP Header

  8. Confirm your settings by clicking Next.

Make sure that the connection test is positive by clicking the icon. In the list of system mappings, the Reachable status should appear in the Check Result column, as shown in Figure 5.53.

Figure 5.53     Example Connection with the Check Status Reachable

To enable RFC calls to the SAP S/4HANA system, you’ll need to repeat these steps. This time, however, you would select the RFC option as the connection type instead of HTTP(S). You may also need to provide other connection data, depending on your backend system (for example, message server or application server).

As a final result, you now have two connections: one RFC connection and one HTTP(S) connection. The Reachable status should be displayed for both connections, as shown in Figure 5.54.

Figure 5.54     Overview of the System Mappings with Both Connections

Now, we have created the basis for configuring the resources. In our example, access is allowed to all resources under the URL path /sap as well as to the function module BAPI_USER_GETDETAIL. For this task, click the plus icon above the list of resources. Add the HTTP resource with all subpaths, as shown in Figure 5.55, as well as the RFC resource for the function module BAPI_USER_GET_DETAIL for the connections created, as shown in Figure 5.56.

Figure 5.55     Adding a Resource for a URL Path

Figure 5.56     Adding a Resource for an ABAP Function Module

Save your resources. As a result, the corresponding destinations are unlocked. To use these resources in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, you still need to create destinations for both cloud-to-on-premise connections, which can be used to address the SAP S/4HANA system. We’ll cover this topic next.

The configuration of the destination for an RFC connection is shown in Figure 5.57. Note the Additional Properties section at the bottom of the screen. In this section, you can define connections to the application server of the SAP S/4HANA system. In the Name field, reference the system using the virtual host name.

Figure 5.57     Configuring a Destination for an RFC Connection

The configuration of the destination for the HTTP connection also refers to the virtual host name, as shown in Figure 5.58. In this case, we’ve added the parameter sap-client in the Additional Properties field to address the correct client immediately.

Figure 5.58     Configuring a Destination for the HTTP Connection

The result is shown in Figure 5.59. We now have a destination HTTPS4DEV for HTTP connections, and a destination RFCS4DEV for RFC connections. Now, cloud applications of SAP BTP can connect to the SAP S/4HANA system in the local company network via the cloud connector.

Figure 5.59     Destinations Created

If the SAP S/4HANA system has not yet been made known to your SAP BTP subaccount, you must import the SSL certificate at this point, as described in Chapter 2, Section 2.1.3. For direct HTTP access, the XSUAA proxy must be used.

5.7.3    LDAP Authentication Configuration

If many people need access to applications connected via the cloud connector, we commend connecting an LDAP-enabled directory service. Configuring authentication via LDAP is one of the main steps in configuring the cloud connector. Therefore, let’s look at this configuration with an example. In this example, we assume the requirement that not only the administration user has to log on to the cloud connector. For example, a developer may have an interest in checking the connections in case of malfunctions. Your company already uses an LDAP directory service, which you would now like to use for logging on to the cloud connector.

The following prerequisites must be met to perform the steps described in this section:

  • You’ve clarified from which paths of the LDAP directory service the users and groups that are to access the cloud connector are to be read.

  • You’ve clarified which attributes are to be mapped.

  • You’ve clarified which system user is allowed to log on via LDAP.

  • You've enabled the technical connection between the cloud connector and the LDAP connector.

First, we’ll configure the cloud connector to authenticate against your LDAP directory service by following these steps:

  1. Log on to the cloud connector and select the Configuration menu area.

  2. On the USER INTERFACE tab, switch to the Authentication block, as shown in Figure 5.60.

    Figure 5.60     Starting an LDAP Connection

  3. Click on the double arrow icon to open the General LDAP Configuration view.

  4. Match the values to those of your LDAP directory service and click Activate, as shown in Figure 5.61.

    Figure 5.61     Configuring LDAP Authentication

  5. Note the settings under Custom Roles, shown in Figure 5.62. In these fields, you can assign your own roles in your LDAP directory to the four default roles of the cloud connector: Administrator Role, Support Role, Display Role, and Monitoring Role. These default roles were described in Section 5.3.

Figure 5.62     Assigning User-Defined Roles to Default Roles

To assign the Display Role to all users, enter “every_user” in the Display Role field. In our example, we have a role named every_user for all users in the LDAP directory service.

The cloud connector will now reboot. You can then log on to the cloud connector using your LDAP credentials. You can click the Test LDAP Configuration icon in the General LDAP Configuration view to test the configuration. If you’ve made a mistake or if logging on is not possible for some other reason, refer to the instructions provided in Section 5.3.

5.8    Summary

In this chapter, you learned about the cloud connector, which links your on-premise and cloud systems. We began by exploring the architecture, and then we walked you through installing and configuring the cloud connector. You learned about several relevant authentication methods, and then we set up and secured some cloud-to-on-premise connections. We then discussed key settings for the SAP Destination service to create destinations in SAP BTP, Neo environment and SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. We also provided a handy checklist for checking your cloud connector configuration. Finally, we explored some real-world examples of secure configuration for the cloud connector, including connecting to an on-premise Git server, connecting to SAP S/4HANA, and connecting using LDAP authentication.

6    Administration Tools

Administering SAP Business Technology Platform (SAP BTP) via the command line or via application programming interfaces (APIs) can help you perform common tasks even more efficiently. You can also integrate SAP BTP into your existing administration tools.

For the most part, SAP BTP is administered via the SAP BTP cockpit. However, there are some tasks that can only be performed using the command-line tools, which are called command-line interfaces (CLIs). In addition, you might have a requirement to automate certain activities or to use existing tools.

SAP offers a command-line tool both in SAP BTP, Neo environment and in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. We’ll show you how to use the tools for SAP BTP, Neo environment in Section 6.1. We covered the command line for SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment in Chapter 4, Section 4.1. The main reason for this placement is that certain functions in this environment can only be administered via the command line. In Section 6.2, we’ll introduce another command-line tool for the administration of global accounts in both SAP BTP environments.

In addition, SAP also provides APIs for certain functions. These APIs are typically expressed as OData APIs and documented in SAP API Business Hub. In Section 6.3, you’ll learn how to access SAP BTP functions using APIs. In Section 6.4, you’ll find a checklist for working with command-line tools and APIs. Finally, in Section 6.5, we walk you through some examples of how to use the command line for certain administration tasks.

[»]  APIs for the Cloud Connector

For information on the APIs for the cloud connector, see Chapter 5, Section 5.2.

6.1    Administering SAP BTP, Neo Environment via the Command Line

The CLI is provided by SAP for download on the SAP Development Tools website at https://tools.hana.ondemand.com, in the Cloud section. Working with the command line differs significantly between SAP BTP, Neo environment and SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. This difference exists because the command line in SAP BTP, Neo environment is a proprietary SAP tool, while a standardized implementation is used for SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment.

We described the installation of the SAP BTP software development kit (SDK) for SAP BTP, Neo environment and the setup of the command line in Chapter 3, Section 3.1. In this section, we’ll present some commands for the command line that, in our experience, are frequently used to administer SAP BTP, Neo environment.

An example call is shown in Figure 6.1.

Figure 6.1     Calling the SAP BTP SDK for SAP BTP, Neo Environment

To access a list of all subaccounts, use the command shown in Listing 6.1. In addition to the technical name of your subaccount, the user name of the subaccount admin and the host are passed.

./neo.sh list-accounts 
       --account <technicalnameofyoursubaccount> 
       --user <SubaccountUser> 
       --host <host>

Listing 6.1     Reading a List of All Subaccounts

Note that you must log on with one of your subaccounts to get a list of all subaccounts.

Alternatively, you have the option of not supplying the parameters directly attached to the command, as in Listing 6.1, but to instead store these parameters in a file. For this task, create a file with all the necessary parameters via a text editor or via the command line itself (on Linux, for example, with the touch command and the vi editor; on Windows with the editor provided). Name the file clouddna.properties. The contents of this file for an example subaccount query is shown in Listing 6.2.

##SubAccount
account=yourSubAccountId
##user
user=yourSubAccount userId
##password
password=yourSubAccountPassword
##host
host=SubAccountHost

Listing 6.2     Contents of a clouddna.properties Parameter File

This file is then called with the following command:

./neo.sh list-accounts clouddna.properties

To get the result in a machine-readable format, you can additionally supply the parameter output with the value json. In this case, you must also supply the password of the subaccount user as well.

Another interesting use case of the Neo console is the creation of a database tunnel between the on-premise landscape and SAP BTP. The SAP HANA service in SAP BTP, Neo environment does not include any tools for direct access to database contents (indirect access via SAP Web IDE is possible). For such access, many developers and administrators prefer to use tools that run on their own computers or in their own system landscapes, such as DataGrip. Such tools use Java Database Connectivity (JDBC) as the protocol for data access. However, the SAP HANA service does not allow a direct database connection using JDBC, for network reasons.

Therefore, as an alternative, the Neo console can be used to connect to a database using the command shown in Listing 6.3.

./neo.sh open-db-tunnel 
       --account <yourSubAccountId> 
       --user <yourSUser> 
       --host eu1.hana.ondemand.com 
       -i <dbInstanceName>

Listing 6.3     Setting Up a Database Tunnel

Again, the data for your subaccount (ID, user, and host) is passed. The only difference from the commands shown so far is the -i parameter. This parameter is the ID of the database or database schema. After the connection has been successfully established, you’ll see the JDBC URL, instance number, and user displayed in the Neo console, as shown in Figure 6.2. You can then use this information in your preferred tool to access the database.

Figure 6.2     Database Tunnel Information

[»]  Setting Up a Service Channel in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment

You can also set up a corresponding connection in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. However, in this case, you would create a service channel in the cloud connector. For more information, refer to SAP Help Portal at http://s-prs.de/v809810.

[+]  Further Commands

A detailed description of further commands for SAP BTP, Neo environment can be found at SAP Help Portal at http://s-prs.de/v809811.

6.2    Managing Global Accounts via the Command Line

The SAP BTP CLI is the command-line tool for all administration tasks related to the global account, subaccounts, and directories. Directories are used to group subaccounts into a directory structure (see also Section 6.5.2). You can use the SAP BTP CLI for both SAP BTP, Neo environment and SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. Note, however, that SAP BTP CLI is used exclusively for managing global accounts, (i.e., the top structural level). In your subaccounts, you’ll need to use the specific command-line tools for either SAP BTP, Neo environment or SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. With the SAP BTP CLI, you can create and update subaccounts and manage authorizations, among other things.

The SAP BTP CLI is installed locally on your computer. Download the tool via the URL https://tools.hana.ondemand.com/#cloud. Navigate to the SAP BTP Command Line Interface (btp CLI) section, where you’ll find different versions for different operating systems, as shown in Figure 6.3. Click on the link for your operating system to start the download.

Figure 6.3     Downloading the SAP BTP CLI

[»]  Prerequisite for Use

To use the SAP BTP CLI, your global account must support feature set B as a cloud management tool. For more information, refer to the beginning of Chapter 4.

To invoke the SAP BTP CLI help, use the following command in the console:

sapcp --help

To work with the console, you must log on to SAP BTP via the command shown in Listing 6.4.

sapcp login 
      --url https://cpcli.cf.eu10.hana.ondemand.com 
      --subdomain <yourSubdomain> 
      --user <yourEmailAddress>

Listing 6.4     Logging on to SAP BTP via the Console

[»]  Logging on to SAP BTP Again

When you log on to SAP BTP from the console, a bearer token is created and stored on your client. This token gives you the ability to close and reopen the console without having to log on again. Each command you execute from the console renews this token. The token is valid for 24 hours.

To read out information about the global account, you can use the following command:

sapcp get accounts/global-account

The result of the call is shown in Figure 6.4.

Figure 6.4     Global Account Information in the SAP BTP CLI

Other commands are entered in the same way. In Section 6.5.2, we’ll cover some commonly used commands for managing SAP BTP with a practical example.

6.3    Administration via APIs

In addition to accessing SAP BTP via the console, SAP also provides APIs for certain activities. Both Representational State Transfer (REST) and OData APIs are offered. Using the APIs, HTTP methods are executed that access a specific URL endpoint. Different APIs are used for SAP BTP, Neo environment and SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment.

[»]  HTTP Methods

For SAP BTP’s REST and OData APIs, the HTTP methods GET, POST, PUT, and DELETE are available:

  • Using the GET method, you can access a record or a list of records in read-only mode.

  • You can use the POST method to create a new record.

  • If you want to modify an existing record, you can use the PUT method. Optionally, you could also use the PATCH or MERGE methods. Which methods are offered depends on the API.

  • To delete an entity, use the DELETE method.

For some APIs, not all of these HTTP methods are offered.

In the following sections, we’ll show you how to locate and test APIs. Due to the wide variety of APIs, knowing how to navigate through the SAP API Business Hub API catalog and how to locate the appropriate APIs is vitally important.

6.3.1    SAP API Business Hub

SAP API Business Hub plays an important role in the SAP BTP environment. Among other things, all APIs are documented in this central location. SAP API Business Hub can be accessed at https://api.sap.com.

In SAP API Business Hub, navigate to the Categories tab, where you’ll find the APIs menu item. You can click the View All Categories link to call up the entire API catalog, as shown in Figure 6.5.

Figure 6.5     SAP API Business Hub: Content Categories

In the API catalog, as shown in Figure 6.6, you’ll find the SAP Cloud Integration tile. Click it to go to the APIs for SAP BTP.

SAP distinguishes between REST, Simple Object Access Protocol (SOAP), and OData APIs. For example, the API for reading the audit logs of SAP BTP is an OData API, and the API for accessing the SAP Destination service is implemented as a REST API. OData is based on REST technology, which means that OData APIs offer extended capabilities, but dealing with them is similar to dealing with REST APIs.

SAP API Business Hub provides you with the ability to test specific APIs directly. Click the SAP Cloud Integration tile and navigate to Integration Content • API Reference. This path takes you to the view shown in Figure 6.7. Now, you can test the Integration Content API for Cloud Integration: Click the Try Out button. Then, maintain the required and optional parameters for the call and make a call directly either against a sandbox account provided by SAP or against your own subaccount of SAP BTP.

Figure 6.6     SAP API Business Hub: Overview of APIs

Figure 6.7     Testing APIs

SAP API Business Hub also provides the option of creating code snippets for specific APIs. If you click on the Code Snippet link in the detailed view of an API, you can access the source code for calling the API in common programming languages. You can also use code snippets generated in this way for a call in the command line. Figure 6.8 shows the generated code for a call to the API endpoint /IntegrationDesigntimeArtifacts using the command-line tool cURL.

Figure 6.8     Code Snippet for Calling an API Endpoint in the Console

[»]  cURL

cURL is a command-line tool that is offered free of charge for all operating systems that can simulate web requests in various protocols. Specific HTTP headers can be included, and the responses viewed, as in the example shown in Figure 6.8. Further information can be found on the cURL website at https://curl.se.

6.3.2    Audit Log Retrieval via APIs

The Audit Log Retrieval API provides you the ability to retrieve audit logs from SAP BTP. This functionality allows you to integrate these audit logs into your own systems and processes, regardless of whether you otherwise use on-premise or cloud-based tools.

Retrieving the Audit Log from SAP BTP, Neo Environment

In SAP BTP, Neo environment, the Audit Log Retrieval API is provided as an OData API. For general information about this API, refer to SAP API Business Hub at https://api.sap.com/api/AuditLogRetrievalAPI/.

Authentication is required to access the audit log data via the API. Authentication in SAP BTP, Neo environment is performed using OAuth. Therefore, as a prerequisite, an OAuth client must be created in the subaccount. Follow these steps:

  1. Open the SAP BTP cockpit of the subaccount and follow the menu path Security • OAuth.

  2. On the OAuth settings screen, open the Platform API tab.

  3. Click on the Create API Client button, as shown in Figure 6.9.

    Figure 6.9     Creating a Platform API Client for OAuth Authentication

  4. In the API client creation dialog box, you’ll need to define the desired scopes, that is, the content that is allowed to be accessed through the API and the APIs that will be used to access it. For our example, select the Audit Log Service in the API Name column and the values Read Audit Logs and Manage Audit Logs in the Scopes column, as shown in Figure 6.10.

  5. Optionally, you can enter a description for the API client in the Description field. Finally, click Save.

  6. The next step displays the credentials for OAuth authentication, as shown in Figure 6.11. The Client ID can still be viewed at a later time, but the Client Secret is only visible at this point. Client ID and Client Secret are used to authenticate the API user. After you’ve noted down the information, confirm with OK.

Figure 6.10     Defining APIs and Scopes for Access

Figure 6.11     Credentials for OAuth Authentication

OAuth authentication is performed via an HTTP POST call to the "/oauth2/apitoken/v1?grant_type=client_credentials endpoint. The SubAccountId must be replaced with the technical name of the subaccount, and the RegionHost must be replaced with the host name of the associated data center. The call must be made with basic authentication; that is, the user name and password are sent in the HTTP header. This information is transmitted using the Base64 encoding method. The OAuth client ID must be used as the user name, and the OAuth client secret must be used as the password.

If authentication is successful, the response is output, as shown in Listing 6.5. The API calls are authenticated using the token_type and access_token attributes included in the response.

{
    "access_token": "2d34fa92f748f7c16048f6ad6d60cc",
    "token_type": "bearer",
    "expires_in": 1500,
    "scope": "hcp.manageAuditLogs hcp.readAuditLogs"
}

Listing 6.5     Response to API Call for OAuth Authentication

[»]  Bearer Token

A bearer token is a special key that is issued once. This token receives an access authorization issued by the respective target system in encrypted form via the access token (as shown in Listing 6.5). Instead of having to send a new combination of user name and password with each request, the token is sent along. It thus serves as a substitute for other authentication methods.

Reading the contents of the audit log is done via an HTTP GET call to the https://api.eu2.hana.ondemand.com/auditlog/v1/accounts/<SubAccountId>/AuditLogRecords endpoint.

The path component <SubAccountId> must also be replaced by the technical name of the subaccount. The HTTP header authorization must be supplied to authenticate the caller. As a value, the previously received token_type must be concatenated with the access_token, separated by a space. In the example response shown in Listing 6.5, the value of the bearer token would correspond to the string 2d34fa92f748f7c16048f6ad6d60cc.

Retrieving the Audit Log of SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment

In SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, calling the audit log is a bit more involved than in SAP BTP, Neo environment. For information about the API, see the Audit Log Retrieval API for SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment in SAP API Business Hub at https://api.sap.com/api/CFAuditLogRetrievalAPI.

The API requires OAuth authentication here as well. The prerequisite for this is the activation of an instance of the SAP Audit Log Management service in SAP BTP:

  1. Navigate to your subaccount in the SAP BTP cockpit and call up the service marketplace via Service • Service Marketplace.

  2. Search for the keyword “Auditlog Management.” Click on the Auditlog Management tile to access the detailed view of the service, as shown in Figure 6.12.

    Figure 6.12     SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment: SAP Audit Log Management Service in the Service Marketplace

  3. Click the Create Instance button to start the process of creating a new service instance, as shown in Figure 6.13.

    Figure 6.13     Creating a Service Instance of the SAP Audit Log Management Service

  4. In the dialog box for configuring and creating the service, enter a descriptive name for your instance in the Instance Name field, as shown in Figure 6.14. You can leave blank fields blank. For other fields, you can leave the default values.

    Figure 6.14     Maintaining Service Information

  5. Continue by clicking Next. Then, click your way through the creation wizard. No further entries should be required. Now, complete the creation of the instance by clicking Create Instance, as shown in Figure 6.15.

Figure 6.15     Completing the Creation of the Instance

You should now receive a success message. In this message, click View Instance to view the details of the service instance, as shown in Figure 6.16.

Figure 6.16     View Service Instance

The next step is to create a service key. For this task, navigate to the Service Keys section in the service instance details view and click the Create button, as shown in Figure 6.17.

Figure 6.17     Creating a Service Key

Enter a suitable technical name for the service key in the Service key name field and complete the creation process by clicking Create, as shown in Figure 6.18.

Figure 6.18     Dialog Box for Creating a Service Key

Open the detailed view of the service key by clicking on its name in the service key overview, as shown in Figure 6.19.

Figure 6.19     Jumping to the Service Key

The service key contains all the information required for authentication and for calling the API for the audit log. Figure 6.20 shows an example of a service key with the relevant properties. The service key is provided in JavaScript Object Notation (JSON) format. The top-level url attribute contains the endpoint for the API call. The uaa object also contains an attribute named url. This attribute is the endpoint used for OAuth authentication. The clientid attribute corresponds to the user name for the API call, and the clientsecret attribute corresponds to the associated password.

Figure 6.20     Structure of a Service Key

OAuth authentication is performed against the endpoint provided in the service key. Additional components must be added to the path of the endpoint. The final endpoint follows the following scheme:

<uaa.url>/oauth/token?grant_type=client_credentials

The call must be sent as an HTTP POST request with basic authentication. The clientid must be passed as the user name, and the clientsecret must be passed as the password. As a result, analogous to the call in SAP BTP, Neo environment, an OAuth response is sent in which the attributes token_type and access_token are supplied. These two attributes are used to authenticate the API calls.

For the API call, the path /auditlog/v2/auditlogrecords must be added to the endpoint. The endpoint should look like the following:

https://auditlog-management.cfapps.eu10.hana.ondemand.com/auditlog/v2/auditlogrecords

The call is sent as an HTTP GET request. For the authentication of the client, the HTTP header authorization must be sent. This information contains the result of the concatenation of token_type and access_token as value, using a space as a separator. An example result of the API call is shown in Figure 6.21.

Figure 6.21     Result of the Audit Log Retrieval API Call

6.3.3    User Management via APIs

We’ve already recommended using a platform identity provider and thus managing the users of the cloud services in a central location. However, to create the users directly in SAP BTP subaccounts, SAP offers a suitable API for this purpose. You can also read out existing user master data via an API.

User Management in SAP BTP, Neo Environment

In SAP BTP, Neo environment, user administration can be performed via the Platform Authorizations Management API. Detailed information about this API can be found in SAP API Business Hub at https://api.sap.com/api/AccountMembersManagementAPI/.

Authentication is performed via OAuth, as discussed for the Audit Log Retrieval API in the previous section. For this purpose, an OAuth client must be created, as described in Section 6.3.2. Assign the scopes Manage Account Members and Read Account Members for the Account Member Management API, as shown in Figure 6.22.

[ ! ]  Attention: Changing the Scopes

The assigned scopes for the OAuth client cannot be changed later. If you want to adjust the scopes, you must delete the existing OAuth client and create a new client with the desired scopes.

OAuth authentication is performed against the https://api.eu2.hana.ondemand.com/oauth2/apitoken/v1?grant_type=client_credentials endpoint.

Figure 6.22     Setting Scopes for the OAuth Client

Calling the API to read all users is performed against the https://api.eu2.hana.ondemand.com/authorization/v1/platform/accounts/<SubAccountId>/Users endpoint.

The <SubAccountId> path element must also be replaced with the technical name of the subaccount at this point. For authentication, the Authorization HTTP header must be passed with the bearer token. The result of an example valid request is shown in Figure 6.23.

Figure 6.23     Result of a User Query via the API

You can use the System for Cross-Domain Identity Management (SCIM) protocol to create users. This protocol is described in SAP API Business Hub at https://api.sap.com/api/AccountMembersManagementAPI/resource. You can test the protocol against your own subaccount at SAP API Business Hub.

User Management in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment

SAP also offers an API for user management in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, called the Authorization Management API. To use this API, the SAP Authorization and Trust Management service must be activated. For this task, navigate to Services • Service Marketplace in the SAP BTP cockpit. Click the Authorization & Trust Management tile in the service overview, as shown in Figure 6.24.

Figure 6.24     SAP Authorization and Trust Management Service in the Service Marketplace of SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment

Starting from the detail view of the service, create a new service instance by clicking the Create Instance button, as shown in Figure 6.25.

Figure 6.25     Creating a Service Instance

The service is delivered in different instances, which are called service plans. For access via the API, an instance must be created with the apiaccess service plan. Select this service plan in the creation dialog box for the service instance, as shown in Figure 6.26.

In the Service Plan field, select the apiaccess plan and assign a descriptive name for the instance in the Instance Name field. Leave the default values in the other required fields. Continue by clicking Next and go through the other steps of the creation process. Finally, click Create Instance.

Figure 6.26     Maintaining the Properties of the Service Instance

Next, create a service key as described in Section 6.3.2. The structure of the service key is slightly different from the service key for the Audit Log Retrieval API. In this case, the endpoint for authentication is contained in the url attribute, as shown in Figure 6.27. As usual, the clientid corresponds to the user name, and the clientsecret corresponds to the password.

Figure 6.27     Service Key for the User Management API

The user is authenticated via OAuth with the client_credentials authentication type (grant_type). If the authentication was successful, you can make SAP BTP calls against the API. Let’s consider an example of reading all the users of your subaccount. The call is made against the https://api.authentication.eu10.hana.ondemand.com/Users endpoint. This call can be made without any additional parameters. Figure 6.28 shows an example response to this request.

Figure 6.28     Result of Calling the User Management API

See https://docs.cloudfoundry.org/api/uaa/version/75.1.0/index.html#users in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment for documentation for all parameters that can be passed to the /Users endpoint.

6.3.4    Permission Management via APIs

For role and permission management, the Authorization Management API is provided, which in our experience is quite commonly used. This API also distinguishes between SAP BTP, Neo environment and SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment.

Authorization Management in SAP BTP, Neo Environment

The documentation for the Authorization Management API for SAP BTP, Neo environment, unlike the APIs presented so far, is not found in SAP API Business Hub, but instead at https://api.hana.ondemand.com/authorization/v1/documentation. This REST API can only be used to manage application roles. You cannot use this API to manage platform roles. The API provides functions for managing roles and assigning roles to users. In addition to roles, it also covers user groups. The application roles are provided by the respective cloud application in the application descriptor. With the application descriptor, developers tell the cloud instance what is to be installed or deployed, including which roles are to be created in the cloud environment for correct operation of the application.

Authentication is performed via OAuth using client credentials. The token issued by the OAuth server has a validity of 25 minutes, and therefore, reauthenticating before each API call is not necessary.

This API is called in the same way as described for the Platform Authorizations Management API in Section 6.3.3. We therefore won’t go into further detail. For more information, see SAP Help Portal at http://s-prs.de/v809813.

Authorization Management in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment

The assignment of roles and authorizations is implemented in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, by the SAP Authorization and Trust Management service. This service comprises a total of four APIs. Figure 6.29 shows that the APIs are provided as REST APIs. Documentation for these APIs can be found in SAP API Business Hub at the URL https://api.sap.com/package/authtrustmgmnt.

Figure 6.29     APIs of the SAP Authorization and Trust Management Service

The Authorization API is used to manage roles and authorizations. An API can provide different entities. In the case of the Authorization API, these entities are applications, roles, role templates, and composite roles, as shown in Figure 6.30.

An example call to the Authorization API might be as follows:

/sap/rest/authorization/v2/apps/<AppId>/roles.

This call would return all the roles that exist for an SAP BTP application <AppId>.

The API calls work in the same way as the APIs presented in Chapter 4, Section 4.4. Therefore, we won’t elaborate further now.

Figure 6.30     Entities of the Authorization API

6.3.5    SAP Cloud Management Service APIs

Administrative activities on SAP BTP can also be performed using APIs from the SAP Cloud Management service for SAP BTP. Similar to using the SAP BTP CLI, this functionality requires that feature set B is available in your global account.

APIs are provided through the service for the following management functions, as shown in Figure 6.31:

  • Management of SAP BTP global accounts and subaccounts (Accounts API)

  • Allocation of resources (Entitlements API)

  • Provisioning of environments, quotas, and job statistics (Provisioning API)

  • Management of events (Events API)

The APIs for the SAP Cloud Management service are also called Core Service APIs for SAP BTP. The current documentation for these APIs can be found at http://s-prs.de/v909814.

SAP Cloud Management service APIs are protected with OAuth 2.0 and via user name and password. For certain APIs, the client authentication method is also used.

Figure 6.31     Core Service APIs

To consume these APIs, you must first activate the SAP Cloud Management service. For this task, in a subaccount of SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, navigate to Services • Service Marketplace and call up the Cloud Management Service tile, as shown in Figure 6.32.

Figure 6.32     SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment: SAP Cloud Management Service in the Service Marketplace

Next, you’ll create a service instance. The service is deployed exclusively in the spaces of SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. Therefore, you must maintain the Space field in the service instance creation dialog box, as shown in Figure 6.33. We recommend that you group all services that are used for the administration of SAP BTP in a separate, dedicated space. Also, select an appropriate technical name for the service instance in the Instance Name field. In the Service Plan field, select the value central, and leave the default values in all other mandatory fields. Then, continue by clicking Next. After you have completed all the steps of the creation wizard, confirm your settings by clicking Create Instance.

Figure 6.33     Creating an Instance of the SAP Cloud Management Service

After the service has been successfully deployed and the instance of the service is running in the selected space, a service key must be generated for this instance. For this task, navigate to the service instances overview screen and select the service instance we just created, as shown in Figure 6.34.

Now, either jump to the details of the service instance using the link in the Service column or open the action menu in the right column of the overview to generate the service key. In the action menu, select the Create Service Key entry, as shown in Figure 6.35. Then, create the service key as described in Section 6.3.2, for SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment.

Figure 6.34     SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry Environment: Overview of Service Instances in the Dev Space

Figure 6.35     Creating a Service Key via the Action Menu

The service key contains all the relevant information you need to call the APIs. Click the name of the service key to access it, as shown in Figure 6.36.

Figure 6.36     Accessing the Service Key

The service key includes the clientid and clientsecret attributes, as in the example shown in Figure 6.37. The clientid corresponds to the user name in OAuth authentication, and the clientsecret corresponds to the password.

Figure 6.37     Showing a Service Key

[»]  OAuth with User Name and Password

In OAuth authentication using user name and password (password credentials), basic authentication is performed via HTTP. For basic authentication, you must use the clientid as the user name and the clientsecret as the password. In addition, you must submit an HTTP body of type x-www-form-urlencoded with the key-value pairs for the grant_type, username, and password attributes. The grant_type key must be assigned the password value, the username key must be assigned the SAP BTP user’s email, and the password key must be assigned the password assigned to that user.

You can test the APIs directly at https://accounts-service.cfapps.eu10.hana.ondemand.com/home using the web-based user interface (UI). This interface is based on the popular Swagger framework for web environments and is great for running and testing web applications and calls to services directly in the browser.

To test an API, select its corresponding tile on the overview screen. In the detailed view of the API, click the Try it out button, as shown in Figure 6.38.

Figure 6.38     Testing an API of the SAP Cloud Management Service

Next, let’s look at how you can use APIs to retrieve information from your global account and from subaccounts with two examples.

To retrieve the global account information, use an HTTP GET request to the path /accounts/v1/globalAccount. The result of the request is shown, for example, in Figure 6.39. In this case, a trial account for SAP BTP was used. In addition to the global key (the guid), you can also query the name (displayName), the creation and modification information (createdDate and modifiedDate), the general status including explanation (entityState and stateMessage), and the Internet subdomain (subdomain). You can also access the contract status; commercial model; consumption-based billing controls and license types (contractStatus, commercialMode, consumptionBased, and licenseType); geographic activation in a region (geoAccess); and contract renewal dates (renewalDate).

Figure 6.39     Response to a Query for Global Account Information

To retrieve subaccount information, use an HTTP GET request to the path /accounts/v1/subaccounts. The result of an example request is shown in Figure 6.40. You’ll find a global key (the GUID) as well as the key of the associated global account (globalAccoungGUID) and the parent node (parentGUID); if the subaccounts are nested in directories, you’ll also see the type of the parent node (parentType).

You can also query the name (displayName), creation and modification information (createdDate and modifiedDate), region (region), and status and general status message (state and stateMessage). In addition, you can retrieve information about the Internet subdomain (subdomain), the activation status of beta functions (betaEnabled), the activation of the production status (usedForProduction), the long description (description), the expiration date (expiryDate), the creator (createdBy), and the zone used (zoneId).

Figure 6.40     Exemplary Response Query of Subaccount Information

6.4    Checklist for Working with the Command Line and APIs

Table 6.1 provides a checklist for working with the administration tools presented in this chapter.

Completed

Task

You’ve determined who gets which administrative permissions for SAP BTP. Derived from this information, only these users should receive similar permissions for the command line. You’ve also defined where API access points and API keys are requested and managed.

You’ve defined where access to SAP BTP applications or via the APIs of SAP BTP applications are documented.

It has been agreed who is allowed to install the command-line tools on the respective company computers.

You’ve considered, especially with the APIs of SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, that some APIs may change frequently (mostly functions are added, rarely changed). Therefore, you must ensure that regular checks are made in SAP API Business Hub to always look for newer and better approaches.

Table 6.1     Checklist for Working with the Command Line and APIs

6.5    Real-World Examples of Using the Command Line Securely

Most administration and setup tasks can be performed in SAP BTP using the web interface. However, many everyday tasks can be performed using the command line, for instance, creating a custom domain, which must be done via the command line. Another example is managing the platform via the command line, which can be quite helpful to enhance automation, for example.

6.5.1    Using the Command Line to Configure a Custom Domain in SAP BTP, Neo Environment

SAP offers you the ability to use a user-defined domain (called a custom domain) on the subaccount level of SAP BTP. CloudDNA GmbH, for example, uses the domain https://www.clouddna.at/ for its subaccount. To set up such a custom domain, different command-line tools are used in SAP BTP, Neo environment versus SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, but the basic procedure is identical. Therefore, in this section, we’ll show you the configuration steps using SAP BTP, Neo environment in our example.

As prerequisites, you must have a basic understanding of the Secure Sockets Layer (SSL) encryption protocol and its successor Transport Layer Security (TLS) before you can start configuring a custom domain (see also Chapter 2, Section 2.1.1). You or your company must own the domain to be used, and the Domain Name System (DNS) server for that domain must be in your access range. The custom domain for SAP BTP, Neo environment can only be set up using the SAP BTP, Neo environment command line, the configuration of which we described in Chapter 3, Section 3.1.

The process starts with the creation of a certificate signing request (CSR). The CSR is created on the command line for SAP BTP, Neo environment, using the command shown in Listing 6.6.

neo generate-csr 
       --account <SubAccountTechnicalName> 
       --user <SubAccountUser>
       --host <SubAccountHost>
       --name <nameofcertificate> 
       --certificate-distinguished-name <certificateDN>

Listing 6.6     Creating a CSR

The name of the domain for which the certificate is to be created must be included as a common name (CN) in the distinguished name (DN) of the certificate.

In the next step, the previously created CSR must be submitted to a certification authority, also called a certificate authority (CA). The CA will sign the certificate. When signing the certificate, specify “F5BigIP” as the server type.

The next step is to import the SSL certificate issued by the CA into the SAP BTP subaccount. The import is performed in the Neo console using the command from Listing 6.7. The certificate must be in Privacy-Enhanced Mail (PEM) format.

neo upload-domain-certificate 
       --account <SubAccountTechnicalName> 
       --user <SubAccountUser>
       --host <SubAccountHost>
       --name <nameofcertificate> 
       --location <pathToCertificate>

Listing 6.7     Importing an SSL Certificate

After the certificate has been successfully imported, it must be bound to the host used in CSR. This action is performed in the Neo console using the command shown in Listing 6.8.

neo bind-domain-certificate 
       --account <SubAccountTechnicalName> 
       --user <SubAccountUser>
       --host <SubAccountHost>
       --ssl-host <SSLHostName> 
       --certificate <nameofcertificate>

Listing 6.8     Binding a Certificate to an SSL Host

To make an application on SAP BTP accessible through the custom domain, you must associate the custom domain with the application URL. For this task, enter the command shown in Listing 6.9 in the Neo console.

neo add-custom-domain 
       --account <SubAccountTechnicalName> 
       --user <SubAccountUser>
       --host <SubAccountHost>
       --custom-domain <CustomDomainHostName>
       --application-url <urlofApplication>
       --ssl-host mysslhostname

Listing 6.9     Associating a Custom Domain with a Cloud Application

The final step is to maintain the DNS records. To route traffic for your custom domain to the desired application on SAP BTP, you must also configure the domain in the DNS you’re using. For this task, you must map the canonical name (CNAME) of your custom domain to the SSL host you have created in the DNS server management tool so that the subdomain can also resolve correctly to the IP address of your server. You must perform this step for each custom domain you want to use.

6.5.2    Using the Command Line to Manage SAP BTP

In Section 6.2, we provided an initial introduction to the SAP BTP CLI, and you learned a few helpful commands. We also pointed out that a logon is mandatory.

After logging on, all commands are executed in the global account by default. To work in a specific subaccount or directory, use the sapcp target command to change the default context, which can be a different global account -ga <ID>, a different subaccount -sa <ID>, or a different directory -dir <ID>. Once you’ve changed contexts, you no longer need to supply the Subaccount or Directory parameters with the calls when executing the commands.

Reading out global account information is quite easy with SAP BTP. We described this command in Section 6.2.

With the following command, you can query a list of all the subaccounts of your global account:

sapcp list accounts/subaccount

In this way, you can determine the ID of a specific subaccount.

If you have a large number of subaccounts, keeping track of them can be difficult. Therefore, SAP has introduced the functionality of directories in feature set B. With a directory, you can structure your subaccounts or set them up hierarchically.

You can create a directory with the command shown in Listing 6.10. We’ll create a directory with the name “SAP PRESS” as an example.

sapcp create accounts/directory 
       --global-account <GlobalAccountSubDomain> 
       --display-name "SAP PRESS"

Listing 6.10     Creating a Directory

The directory is then available in the SAP BTP cockpit in the Directories area. It does not yet contain any subaccounts or regions, as shown in Figure 6.41.

[»]  Creating a Directory via the Web Interface

Under the Directories menu item on the right, you can create directories (via Create Directory, as shown in Figure 6.41) and manage them accordingly. You can set which properties should apply in the stored subaccounts and then place subaccounts in the directories. This capability is intended to enable you to further structure your subaccounts, especially when the system landscape becomes more complex.

Figure 6.41     SAP BTP Cockpit: Directory Overview

Now that you have an empty directory, you can move a subaccount into the directory. For this task, use the move command, the syntax of which is shown in Listing 6.11. Both the ID of the subaccount and the ID of the directory must be supplied.

sapcp [OPTIONS] move accounts/subaccount ID 
       --global-account SUBDOMAIN 
       --to-directory ID

Listing 6.11     Moving a Subaccount to a Directory

[+]  Calling Help for a Command

You do not need to know the syntax of all commands and all their associated parameters by heart. Use the following command, for example, to call the SAP BTP CLI help for the move command:

sapcp --help move accounts/subaccount

If you do not have the IDs of the subaccount and the directory, use the following command to first read the global account and pass the show hierarchy parameter:

sapcp get accounts/global-account 
       --show-hierarchy

This command will provide you with all the IDs of the subaccounts and directories in addition to the global account information.

After you’ve moved the subaccount to the directory via the command line, the tile of your directory will be displayed in the SAP BTP cockpit, as shown in Figure 6.42. A subaccount and region are now specified as content. Note that there is a 1:1 mapping between subaccount and region.

Figure 6.42     Directory with Assigned Subaccount

You can also view a hierarchical representation of your global account, as shown in Figure 6.43, using the buttons next to the search dialog box.

Figure 6.43     Hierarchical Representation of a Global Account

Let’s now introduce you to two more useful commands for managing subaccounts. With the following command, you can query a list of all composite roles within a subaccount:

sapcp list security/role-collection 
       --subaccount <SubAccountID>

Use the following command to retrieve all the available service plans within a subaccount:

sapcp list services/plan 
       --subaccount <SubAccountID>

At this point, you’ve learned the most important commands for the administration of SAP BTP accounts. As mentioned, many more commands exist, but presenting them in detail would go beyond the scope of this book.

6.6    Summary

In this chapter, you learned about the administration tools provided by SAP BTP. To start, we covered using the SAP BTP CLI for the administration of SAP BTP, Neo environment and for managing global accounts. We then walked you through administration tasks using APIs, including audit log retrieval, user management, and permission management. We then provided a handy checklist for working with the CLI and APIs. Finally, we explored two real-world examples of using the CLI, including configuring a custom domain and managing SAP BTP.

7    Securing Key Cloud Services

SAP Business Technology Platform (SAP BTP) provides a wide range of services and subscriptions. In many cases, the individual services have rather different requirements regarding their security. In this chapter, we’ll look at the specific requirements for some frequently used services.

This chapter covers cloud security topics. We’ll break down our coverage into the following areas:

  • Development
    In this chapter, you’ll learn how to securely deploy and operate some commonly used scenarios. The development of user interfaces (UIs), backend functionalities, workflows, and mobile applications is the most prominent use case for SAP BTP. SAP initially introduced SAP Web IDE as a development environment in SAP BTP, Neo environment for this purpose. After the introduction of SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, this development environment was renamed SAP Web IDE, full stack, as no equivalent environment was available in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment.
    However, soon SAP would also provide a development environment in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. In this environment, the decision was made not to continue SAP Web IDE and instead to develop SAP Business Application Studio, which is thus the logical successor to SAP Web IDE. SAP Business Application Studio offers an advantage in that all plug-ins provided for SAP Business Application Studio can also be used locally in the popular Visual Studio Code (VS Code) development environment. We’ll show you in Section 7.1 what you need to consider when securing SAP Web IDE and SAP Business Application Studio.

  • Integration
    In addition to the development of applications, the topic of integration plays an important role in the context of SAP applications. SAP has provided the Cloud Integration tool as a counterpart to the on-premise middleware SAP Process Orchestration in the cloud. Originally, this service was introduced under the name SAP HANA Cloud Integration and continued for a long time under the name SAP Cloud Platform Integration. With the transition from SAP Cloud Platform to SAP BTP, the tool received the current name, Cloud Integration.

    Cloud Integration is an Apache Camel-based solution that has been absorbed as a service into SAP Integration Suite. Apache Camel is a free coding project of the Apache Foundation. The framework can be used for routing and conversion capabilities in Java applications. SAP has extended this project. In Section 7.2, you’ll learn about security aspects of using Cloud Integration within SAP Integration Suite. Since a large proportion of existing customers still use Cloud Integration in SAP BTP, Neo environment, we’ll also address the special features of this environment.

    The provision of programming interfaces, generally called application programming interfaces (APIs), is often included in the context of integrations. This feature enables applications to be integrated into existing solutions and to access them via standardized protocols and technologies. APIs should not be provided arbitrarily, but via a central service instead. For this purpose, SAP offers the SAP API Management service, which is also part of SAP Integration Suite and can now no longer be licensed as a standalone solution. We’ll discuss the security aspects of this service in Section 7.3.

  • User experience (UX)
    SAP Fiori applications can be deployed via the SAP Fiori launchpad not only in your on-premise landscape, but also on SAP BTP. For this purpose, SAP provides the SAP Launchpad service in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. For SAP BTP, Neo environment, these functions are integrated into the SAP Cloud Portal service. In Section 7.4, you’ll learn what to look out for from a security perspective when using these services.

  • ABAP goes cloud
    Finally, we wanted to look at SAP BTP, ABAP environment. With this ABAP development and runtime environment based in the cloud, SAP has hit its mark with its existing customers. Until introducing SAP BTP, ABAP environment, SAP’s customers and partners had to rely on Java, Node.js, and Python to develop and extend cloud applications. This limitation resulted in the need to rebuild relevant skills and knowledge in many companies. With ABAP development for cloud applications, SAP customers and partners can now build on their existing ABAP skills and at the same time make themselves fit for SAP S/4HANA and the world of the cloud. We therefore address the security aspects of SAP BTP, ABAP environment in Section 7.5.

Finally, in Section 7.6, you’ll learn how to set up a corporate user store as a source for SAP BTP users. Finally, in Section 7.7, we provide a checklist for you to use when securing cloud services.

7.1    SAP Web IDE and SAP Business Application Studio

SAP Web IDE, full stack, is the development environment and tool in SAP BTP, Neo environment. SAP Business Application Studio is the logical successor of SAP Web IDE and is provided only in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. Migration of applications developed on SAP Web IDE to SAP Business Application Studio is most easily accomplished using Git repositories. In the following sections, we address security aspects of both development environments.

7.1.1    SAP Web IDE

SAP Web IDE is used in many ways in SAP BTP. It is used for the development of SAPUI5 and SAP Fiori applications as well as for the development of workflows or Internet of Things (IoT) applications. SAP Web IDE can be extended by various plug-ins.

For development with SAP Web IDE, you need authorizations for the following software components to cover the full lifecycle in application development and deployment:

  • Git server for version control

  • Optionally, a continuous integration (CI)/continuous delivery (CD) service for the central build and deployment of the applications. For example, the SAP Continuous Integration and Delivery service or Microsoft Azure DevOps Services can be used for these functionalities in the cloud, and Apache Jenkins can be used on-premise.

  • A system on which the applications are to be provided. For example, SAP BTP can be used here, but you can also use an on-premise system such as SAP S/4HANA or SAP Business Suite.

The authorizations for the Git server or the repositories behind it are usually not managed in SAP BTP. However, one exception exists: If you use the SAP Git service of SAP BTP, Neo environment, its permissions are also managed at the level of the corresponding subaccount.

[»]  Git on SAP BTP

SAP offers the SAP Git service in SAP BTP, Neo environment at the subaccount level, but not in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. Therefore, at the time of this writing, when using the functionalities of SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, you must resort to an external Git provider such as GitHub, GitLab, or BitBucket or host a Git server in your system landscape yourself.

Regardless of whether you use Git, SAP Web IDE offers the possibility to work based on workspaces. These areas allow development teams to encapsulate related developments. Developers who have experience with the Eclipse development environment are familiar with this concept, as it has been proven in Eclipse over the decades.

To see SAP Web IDE roles, you’ll need to jump to the configuration from the SAP Web IDE service overview screen. For this task, click on the Configure Service link, as shown in Figure 7.1.

Figure 7.1     SAP Web IDE: Service Overview

As shown in Figure 7.2, SAP Web IDE comes with three standard roles: DiDeveloper, DiAdministrator, and DiScpGitAdmistrator. These roles can be found in SAP BTP, Neo environment in the SAP BTP cockpit, under the Roles area.

Figure 7.2     SAP Web IDE: Roles

The permissions provided with these roles are described in Table 7.1.

Roles

Description

DiDeveloper

Implementation of developments with SAP Web IDE, full stack

DiAdministrator

Management of user data, export of user workspaces, and deletion of user data

DiScpGitAdministrator

Management of Git repositories and push commits from other developers

Table 7.1     SAP Web IDE: Standard Roles for Development

Basically, you have two options for assigning roles: Either a user or a user group is assigned directly to a role. Figure 7.3 shows the two variants using the DIDeveloper role as an example. We recommend that you work with groups. Among other things, you can also use groups in mappings for identity providers.

Figure 7.3     Assigning Roles to Individual Users or Groups

If you decide to use user groups, in the SAP BTP cockpit, navigate to the Authorization Management screen, where you can assign both roles and users to these groups. Figure 7.4 shows the assignment options available using the CloudDNA Developers group as an example.

Figure 7.4     Creating and Managing User Groups

Additionally, you have the option of performing role assignments to groups through mappings based on the Security Assertion Markup Language (SAML) assertion of an identity provider, as shown in Figure 7.5. In this case, the identity provider must submit the required attributes in the SAML response. For more information about provisioning users and groups in SAP BTP, Neo environment via identity providers, see Chapter 3, Section 3.3.

Figure 7.5     Identity Provider Group Mapping

SAP Web IDE also uses the SAP Connectivity service from SAP BTP in the background, for example, to access the data of an on-premise system via OData services. However, the connections used must be set up in the subaccount to be usable.

7.1.2    SAP Business Application Studio

SAP Business Application Studio is offered by SAP as a cloud service at the hyperscalers Amazon, Microsoft, Google, and Alibaba in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. As with SAP Web IDE, SAP Business Application Studio is a web-based development tool.

Developers can create what are called dev spaces in SAP Business Application Studio, and these spaces can be personalized and are provided completely isolated from the dev spaces of other users. SAP Business Application Studio is provided in the form of a multitenant application (i.e., the service must be subscribed to via a subscription). Only subscriptions to the Identity Authentication service and the SAP Destination service run in the subaccount; the others, such as SAP Business Application Studio, run as multitenant applications. The SAP Destination service enables the cloud-based applications you develop and use to access other cloud-based and on-premise systems.

The basic architecture of cloud application delivery via SAP Business Application Studio is shown in Figure 7.6.

Figure 7.6     SAP Business Application Studio: Architecture

You can find SAP Business Application Studio by navigating to Services • Instances and Subscriptions in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, as shown in Figure 7.7. Click the SAP Business Application Studio link to access the details. If you have not yet subscribed to the service, you can do so by clicking the Create button.

Figure 7.7     SAP Business Application Studios: Subscribing

Under Security • Role Collections of the subaccount, you’ll see three predelivered role templates, as shown in Figure 7.8:

  • Business_Application_Studio_Developer

  • Business_Application_Studio_Administrator

  • Business_Application_Studio_Extension_Deployer

Figure 7.8     SAP Business Application Studio: Role Templates

The role collection Business_Application_Studio_Developer is the counterpart to the DiDeveloper role in SAP Web IDE and authorizes development in SAP Business Application Studio. The role Business_Application_Studio_Administrator, the counterpart to the role DiAdministrator in SAP Web IDE, allows you to manage and export dev spaces. Only the role template Business_Application_Studio_Extension_Deployer, used to develop and install extensions, lacks a counterpart in SAP Web IDE.

The role collections for SAP Business Application Studio do not have to be created manually. They are created automatically when the service is activated. To assign a role collection to a user, go to the user details screen and click the Assign Role Collection button, as shown in Figure 7.9.

Assigning role collections to users or user groups must be done individually, as shown in Figure 7.10. Select a role collection from the list and confirm by clicking Assign Role Collection. The roles are not mutually exclusive and do not overlap in terms of the permissions they contain. Therefore, you can assign all available roles to a user. We also recommend at this point to assign the role collections by a mapping based on the SAML assertion via an identity provider.

Figure 7.9     Assigning Role Collections

Figure 7.10     Assign Role Collections Dialog Box

7.2    Cloud Integration

Cloud Integration is one of the most widely used services of SAP BTP. This solution is offered in both SAP BTP, Neo environment and SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. In SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment, Cloud Integration is part of SAP Integration Suite and is bundled with other products.

[+]  SAP Integration Suite

SAP Integration Suite combines the integration functions from SAP Process Integration (the Cloud Integration service), SAP API Management, Integration Advisor, Open Connectors, and Trading Partner Management (TPM) in a coherent and simplified toolkit. SAP Integration Suite provides comprehensive enterprise application integration capabilities. Starting in 2021, these services are no longer offered separately and are only available as part of a service plan for SAP Integration Suite.

In terms of architecture, some fundamental differences exist between the Cloud Integration service in SAP BTP, Neo environment and the service in SAP BTP, Cloud Foundry environment. Therefore, in this section, we’ll first compare the architectures and special features of the two environments before going into the management of roles and permissions.

7.2.1    Cloud Integration in SAP BTP, Neo Environment

Cloud Integration is provided within SAP BTP, Neo environment, in the form of a subscription. As a multitenant application, Cloud Integration is operated by SAP in what’s called a provider account and consumed by customers in their own subaccounts. Data storage, authentication, and connectivity are completely isolated from other accounts at the consumer level.

Figure 7.11     SAP BTP, Neo Environment: Cloud Integration Architecture

The service consists of a tenant management node (TMN) and one or more worker nodes, often called integration flow mappers (IFLMAPs). Dialog users work exclusively with the TMN, while interface calls run against the IFLMAP node(s).
